Discovery of Van Hove Singularities: Electronic Fingerprints of 3Q Magnetic Order in a van der Waals Quantum Magnet

Experiment 1

Single crystals of cobalt-intercalated tantalum disulfide, CoxTaS2, are prepared to provide a composition-tunable van der Waals magnetic platform in which Co atoms occupy interlayer sites and form a triangular superlattice coupled to TaS2 conduction layers. For each growth batch, elemental Co, Ta, and S are combined in the nominal molar ratio Co:Ta:S = x:1:2, with x chosen in the range 0.29 to 0.36. The mixture is loaded into alumina crucibles, sealed in quartz tubes under 200 torr partial pressure of Ar, heated to 900 °C, held for 10 days, then furnace-cooled naturally to form a polycrystalline precursor powder. The precursor powder for each batch is ground separately, loaded with iodine transport agent into an evacuated quartz tube, and placed in a horizontal two-zone furnace. The precursor plus iodine are placed in zone 1 and the growth end of the tube in zone 2. Both zones are heated to 850 °C for 6 h for nucleation; then zone 1 is raised to 950 °C while zone 2 remains at 850 °C for 10 days, followed by natural furnace cooling. The goal is to determine whether this chemical-vapor-transport recipe forms macroscopic hexagonal CoxTaS2 crystals over the intended Co-composition interval. Energy-dispersive X-ray spectroscopy using an Oxford Instruments Xplore 30 detector is used to determine the Co composition for each crystal by measuring 10 distinct real-space regions per single crystal and reporting the average and standard deviation; the paper does not report the individual region sizes or probe depth.

Experiment 2

Low-temperature angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy is used to compare the electronic structure of pristine 2H-TaS2 with Co-intercalated Co0.32TaS2, addressing the central question of how Co intercalation modifies TaS2-derived bands and whether additional near-Fermi-level Co-derived electronic states appear. Measurements are performed at Beamline 7.0.2 MAESTRO of the Advanced Light Source using a Scienta Omicron R4000 hemispherical analyzer. The pristine 2H-TaS2 single crystal is a purchased 2D Semiconductors sample; the Co0.32TaS2 sample is grown by the chemical-vapor-transport procedure described in the paper. Data for Co0.32TaS2 are acquired at photon energy hν = 79 eV with linearly horizontal polarization; data for pristine 2H-TaS2 are acquired at hν = 93 eV with linearly horizontal polarization. All spectra are measured at T = 7 K. The momentum-space scope includes Fermi-surface maps spanning the first Brillouin-zone region labeled by Γ0, K0, and M0 for the 1×1 TaS2 lattice and Γ, K, and M for the √3×√3 Co superlattice, plus energy-momentum cuts along M0–Γ0–K0. For Co0.32TaS2, spectra are taken from TaS2-terminated surface regions, with surface terminations differentiated by spatially resolved core-level spectroscopy; the paper does not report the real-space beam spot size or the dimensions of the selected termination domains. Fermi-surface sheets are extracted from the raw momentum maps, and band dispersions are obtained by fitting Lorentzian-shaped momentum-distribution-curve and energy-distribution-curve peak positions. The measurement is configured to quantify band shifts, spin-orbit momentum splitting, and the presence and energy positions of shallow near-EF bands associated with the Co triangular lattice.

Experiment 3

Polarization-dependent ARPES is performed on TaS2-terminated Co0.32TaS2 to distinguish orbital characters of near-Fermi-level Co-derived bands from TaS2-derived bands, which is central to identifying the electronic fingerprints of the Co triangular magnetic lattice. Measurements are carried out at the Advanced Light Source MAESTRO beamline with hν = 79 eV and a Scienta Omicron R4000 analyzer at T = 7 K. Energy-momentum cuts are acquired along the M0–Γ0–K0 high-symmetry direction using linearly horizontal and linearly vertical photon polarizations. The momentum-space scope covers the Γ0-centered cut through adjacent M0 and K0 points; the selected real-space surface is TaS2-terminated, identified by spatially resolved core-level spectroscopy, but the paper does not report the ARPES spot size or the lateral size of the surface domain. The measurement uses polarization matrix-element contrast, together with orbital-projected calculations, to assign which atomic orbitals dominate the α, β, γK, γM, and ε bands, and to test for any localized nondispersive states in the same energy window.

Experiment 4

Composition-dependent ARPES is used to determine how changing Co content across the reported magnetic boundary near xc ≈ 0.33 affects TaS2-derived bands and Co-derived near-EF bands in CoxTaS2. The central concept is that Co intercalation both dopes the host TaS2-derived bands and creates Co-derived flat bands whose response may be tied to 3Q magnetic order and van Hove singularities. Single crystals with EDS-determined compositions x = 0.29, 0.31, 0.33, 0.34, and 0.36 are measured at T = 7 K at the Advanced Light Source MAESTRO beamline using hν = 79 eV and linearly horizontal polarization. Energy-momentum spectra are acquired along the M0–Γ0–K0 high-symmetry direction on TaS2-terminated regions; the real-space beam size and domain dimensions are not reported. Energy distribution curves at the M point are extracted from the same cuts to determine the α, β, and γM peak positions, while γK band-bottom positions are extracted from EDCs at K. The independent variable is Co stoichiometry x across 0.29–0.36. The measurement is configured to quantify relative band-energy shifts of Ta-derived and Co-derived states while holding the ARPES geometry and low-temperature condition fixed.

Experiment 5

In situ potassium-deposition ARPES is performed on a single Co0.29TaS2 crystal to distinguish intrinsic composition trends from possible sample-to-sample variations and to test the response of the near-EF Co-derived γM band to electron donation at a fixed underlying crystal composition. The central idea is that alkali-metal atoms deposited on the exposed surface donate electrons and tune the chemical potential without changing the bulk Co stoichiometry or magnetic-ion arrangement. A TaS2-terminated Co0.29TaS2 surface is measured at T = 7 K at the Advanced Light Source MAESTRO beamline using hν = 79 eV; spectra are collected along Γ0–K0 before potassium deposition and after successive deposition rounds labeled first through fifth. The paper does not report deposition rate, deposition time, K coverage, real-space beam spot size, or the lateral size of the probed surface region. EDCs at the M point are used to track α, β, and γM peak positions after each deposition step. The independent variable is potassium deposition number, with the same sample and ARPES geometry retained across the series.

Experiment 6

Doping-dependent Fermi-surface ARPES maps of CoxTaS2 are acquired to test for momentum-space fingerprints of the transition between the low-doping 3Q magnetic regime and the higher-doping helical regime. The central mechanism being probed is that a Co triangular-lattice band near three-quarters filling can host van Hove singularities, and 3Q magnetic order modifies the Fermi-surface topology and redistributes spectral weight between the tips of γ pockets and the M point. Single crystals with Co compositions spanning x = 0.29 to 0.36 are measured at T = 7 K with hν = 79 eV on TaS2-terminated surfaces at the Advanced Light Source. The momentum-space scope includes Fermi-surface intensity maps around the Co-superlattice Brillouin zone, including K and M points, with representative maps for x = 0.29, 0.32, and 0.34 and extracted γ-pocket contours for the full composition series. Momentum-distribution curves along the K–M direction are compared for x = 0.29 and x = 0.36. The spectral-weight metric is defined as the ARPES Fermi-surface intensity at the tip of the γ pocket divided by the intensity at the M point, using the same Fermi-surface mapping convention. The paper does not report the real-space beam spot size, scan area, or number of repeated cleaves for each composition.

Experiment 7

ARPES band-dispersion cuts along the Co-superlattice K–M–K′ direction are measured as a function of Co composition to directly test the central proposed electronic fingerprint of 3Q magnetic order: reconstruction of the near-EF Co-derived band into an inverse-Mexican-hat-like dispersion with van-Hove-related band extrema displaced away from M. CoxTaS2 single crystals with x = 0.29, 0.32, 0.33, 0.34, and 0.36 are measured at T = 7 K using hν = 79 eV at the Advanced Light Source on TaS2-terminated surfaces. The momentum-space scope is a one-dimensional cut through K–M–K′ of the Co-superlattice Brillouin zone; second-derivative images with respect to energy are derived from the raw spectra, and EDC stacks are analyzed at selected momenta including the γ-band Fermi momentum kF and the M point. The spectral-weight metric is defined as the ratio of the EDC peak intensity at kF to the EDC peak intensity at M along the same cut. Band dispersions for x = 0.29 and x = 0.36 are extracted from EDC peak positions; the dot size in the plotted extraction encodes relative EDC peak intensity. The real-space beam spot size, lateral homogeneity length, and number of repeated sample locations are not reported. The independent variable is Co composition x, and the measurement targets the low-energy Co-derived band curvature and spectral-weight transfer across the reported magnetic-regime boundary near xc ≈ 0.33.

Device-independent quantum memory certification in two-point measurement experiments

2601.14191
Prediction Quality0.7837

Formula Accuracyn/a

Experiment 1

Device-independent two-point-measurement certification of a local qubit quantum memory is implemented on a trapped-ion processor to test whether temporal correlations can rule out a classical, entanglement-breaking memory model without trusting device internals. The processor consists of a linear chain of three ground-state-cooled 40Ca+ ions in a macroscopic linear Paul trap; the two outer ions encode qubits A′ and E, while the middle ion is a spacer to reduce optical addressing cross-talk over the ion-chain length. Qubit manipulation uses an individually addressed 729 nm laser with linewidth below 1 Hz incident at 67.5 degrees to the trap axis through a crossed-AOD addressing system; state readout uses resonant 397 nm fluorescence collected on an EMCCD camera, with fluorescence corresponding to population in the S1/2 manifold and absence of fluorescence to population in the D5/2 manifold. The sequence prepares the two outer ions A′+E in a Bell state using a Mølmer-Sørensen entangling gate. Qubit E is spectroscopically shelved into unused D5/2 levels during the mid-circuit measurement on A′ to reduce measurement back-action. The first measurement setting X is sampled from four Pauli choices {σx, σz, −σx, −σz}, with binary outcome A. A measure-and-prepare instrument is implemented so that the post-measurement qubit A is prepared in a σx eigenstate depending only on A and not on X; in the memory-test assignment, outcome A=1 corresponds to |+⟩ and A=0 corresponds to |−⟩, with equivalent sign conventions handled by post-processing of the four X settings. After readout, Doppler and polarization-gradient recooling are applied because the fluorescence measurement heats the shared motional mode; resolved sideband recooling is not used after the mid-circuit measurement in order not to disturb E. With no deliberately added storage-delay sweep in this certification run, qubits A+E undergo a CNOT gate followed by a virtual full SWAP, where the CNOT is composed of one Mølmer-Sørensen interaction and local rotations R^C_0(π/2), R^T_π(π/2), R^T_{π/4}(π), MS^C,T_XX(π/2), and R^T_{π/2}(π/2). The second-time qubit B is measured in the (σx+σz)/√2 basis. Repeated runs over the four X settings produce the empirical probabilities Pr(A,B|X). The device-independent memory witness is Γ = Σ_{b0,b1} inf_{x∈{0,1,2,3}} Σ_{a∈{0,1}} Pr(A=a,B=b_a|X=x), whose classical instrumental bound is Γ≥1. The reported qubit-memory quality is the Uhlmann fidelity FMemory between the Choi-Jamiołkowski state of the implemented memory channel and that of an ideal identity memory, lower-bounded from Γ using FMemory ≥ 1/2[1 − (Γ − 2 + S_K)/(√2 − S_K)], with S_K=(8+7√2)/17. To assess cross-talk in the same spatially local ion-chain geometry, the Pearl parameter Δ = max_a Σ_b sup_x Pr(A=a,B=b|X=x) is computed, and the average causal direct effect is defined as ACDE = sup |Pr(B_do(A=a,X=x)=b) − Pr(B_do(A=a,X=x′)=b)| over the indicated binary outcomes and settings; in this implementation the same empirical probability table and post-processed intervention assignments are used to estimate these quantities.

Experiment 2

Temporal classicalisation of the same device-independent two-point-measurement qubit-memory protocol is characterized by sweeping a storage waiting time between the mid-circuit measurement-and-repreparation step and the CNOT-plus-virtual-SWAP readout operation. The physical platform, spatial geometry, optical addressing, shelving of the E ion, 397 nm EMCCD fluorescence readout, recooling after the first measurement, first measurement settings X∈{σx, σz, −σx, −σz}, σx-eigenstate post-measurement preparation of qubit A, and final B measurement in the (σx+σz)/√2 basis are the same as in the no-delay certification sequence. The added waiting time is swept from 0 ms to 100 ms. During this interval, dynamical decoupling echo pulses are inserted every 2.5 ms to suppress quasi-static and 100 Hz magnetic-field noise, while active magnetic-field compensation suppresses the 50 Hz mains component. For every waiting time, repeated experimental shots over all four X settings provide Pr(A,B|X), from which the rescaled violation of the classicality inequality is computed; positive rescaled values correspond to a violation of the classical temporal-causal bound, and the certification duration is determined from the waiting-time range over which the data still certify a non-classical qubit memory under the stated protocol.

Experiment 3

A two-point-measurement experiment is performed on the same three-ion 40Ca+ trapped-ion processor to test non-classical causal relations by coherently varying the balance between common-cause and direct-cause mechanisms. The two outer ions encode A′ and E and the middle ion is a spacer; qubit control uses the 729 nm addressed laser at 67.5 degrees, and readout uses 397 nm fluorescence imaging on an EMCCD. The outer ions are initially prepared in a Bell state by a Mølmer-Sørensen gate. The first measurement on A′ uses the same four sampled Pauli settings X∈{σx, σz, −σx, −σz} with binary outcome A, while E is shelved during the mid-circuit measurement and the ion chain is recooled afterward. In this causal-relation protocol, the measure-and-prepare state of A is a σy eigenstate depending only on A: |+i⟩ for A=0 and |−i⟩ for A=1, with sign conventions handled by the four-setting post-processing table. The two-qubit operation after the first measurement is the partial-swap gate U_PS(α)=cos(α/2) id + i sin(α/2) U_swap, implemented as MS_ZZ(−α/2)·MS_YY(−α/2)·MS_XX(−α/2), where the rotation angle is controlled by laser intensity while keeping the gate time fixed. The independent control parameter α is swept over the interval 0 to π radians. The final measurement of B is σx. For each α, repeated runs over all X settings yield Pr(A,B|X), from which the same classical causal inequality witness is evaluated to determine whether the observed process can be simulated as a classical mixture of common-cause and direct-cause processes.

Experiment 4

The effective coherence time relevant to the trapped-ion quantum-memory decay model is characterized independently with Ramsey-type measurements on the same 40Ca+ trapped-ion processor. The qubit is manipulated with the addressed 729 nm laser and read out by 397 nm fluorescence imaging, while the magnetic environment is treated as in the memory-decay experiment: active compensation suppresses coherent 50 Hz mains magnetic-field noise, and a dynamical-decoupling echo sequence with pulses separated by 2.5 ms suppresses quasi-static and 100 Hz components. Ramsey experiments are run over a waiting-time sweep spanning 0 ms to 200 ms, and the Ramsey contrast is fit with an exponential decay model corresponding to Lorentzian-distributed frequency noise. The fitted decay constant is used as the effective T2 coherence time of the trapped-ion qubit under the dynamical-decoupling conditions used for the quantum-memory certification sequence.

Experiment 1

Central concept: quantify the hBN dielectric response used to convert applied voltage in the monolayer CrI3 vertical device into out-of-plane electric field and electrostatic doping, which are the control knobs for voltage-tunable DMI and magnetic anisotropy. The hBN dielectric layer is part of a graphene–hBN–CrI3–graphene/graphite device stack on SiO2/Si. Its thickness is determined by optical contrast on an hBN–SiO2–Si structure: white light is normally incident and focused with a 50×, NA = 0.45 microscope objective, reflected light is collected through a beamsplitter into a spectrometer, and a CCD records the spectrally resolved reflected signal. The optical contrast is defined as C = (R_SiO2 − R_SiO2+hBN)/R_SiO2, where R_SiO2 is the normal-incidence reflection coefficient of bare SiO2/Si and R_SiO2+hBN is the coefficient for hBN-covered SiO2/Si. The relative dielectric constant of the hBN dielectric is measured using a ferroelectric analyzer; electrode geometry, AC amplitude, and frequency are not specified in the provided text. The spatial scope is the dielectric flake used in the gated device; the exact mapped area and number of measurement points are not reported.

Experiment 4

Central concept: test voltage creation and continuous manipulation of topological spin textures in the strict monolayer CrI3 limit by using reflective topological circular dichroism, an optical-frequency analogue of the topological Hall effect. RCDT peak positions are extracted by Gaussian fitting and plotted versus gate voltage. The measurement is made on a designated confocal spot/active region of the device; exact lateral spot diameter, spatial map area, and number of independent devices or positions are not reported. The phase-mapping objective is to determine the voltage range in which nontrivial topological magnetic order is optically detected and how its characteristic field shifts with voltage as magnetic anisotropy evolves from perpendicular toward in-plane.

Fundamental Tests of Quantum Geometric Bounds in Ionic and Covalent Insulators using Inelastic X-Ray Scattering

Experiment 1

Central concept: inelastic x-ray scattering (IXS) is used as a direct density-response probe from which the quantum Fisher information density f_Q, Bures metric g_q, and longitudinal quantum weight K(q) of insulating solids can be obtained, enabling comparison of quantum-information delocalization in covalently bonded diamond and ionically bonded LiF. The experiment uses room-temperature IXS at Sector 9-ID of the Advanced Photon Source. A liquid-nitrogen-cooled Si(111) primary monochromator and Si(333) secondary channel-cut prepare the incident beam. For diamond, spectra are collected at incident photon energy 8981.8 eV using a Ge(733) backscattering analyzer with 0.3 eV energy resolution and momentum transfer q parallel to crystallographic direction (1,-1,0). For LiF, spectra are collected at incident photon energy 6482.5 eV using a Ge(531) analyzer with 140 meV energy resolution and q parallel to (1,0,0). The nominal momentum resolution is about 0.15 Å^-1. Energy-loss scans are obtained by scanning the incident energy over electronic excitations up to 100 eV; the plotted momentum-transfer sequence spans approximately q = 0.32 to 4.55 Å^-1. The quasi-elastic contribution below about 3 eV is removed, and the remaining spectra are treated as the dynamic structure factor/density response for electronic excitations above the band gaps. The spatial sampling area, beam footprint, number of probed sample positions, and sample mosaic/defect mapping are not specified in the text; the geometric scope explicitly reported is the reciprocal-space direction and momentum-resolution window. The spectra are converted to χ''(q,ω) in physical units by enforcing the f-sum rule after extrapolating intensity above the 100 eV cutoff as I(q,ℏω>Ω)=c(q)Ω^3/(ℏω)^3, where Ω=100 eV and c(q) is the average spectral intensity from 94 to 100 eV at fixed q. The partial f-sum normalization uses eight active electrons per primitive diamond unit cell with primitive lattice constant 2.52 Å and ten active electrons per LiF primitive unit cell with primitive lattice constant 2.84 Å. The analysis computes g_q=-(ℏ/π)∫_0^∞dω tanh(βω/2)χ''(q,ω) and f_Q=4g_q at room temperature, with β the inverse thermal energy; for the high-energy electronic spectrum the Bose factor is effectively unity.

Experiment 2

Central concept: the same IXS-derived density response can be Kramers-Kronig transformed to obtain the static electronic longitudinal dielectric response needed to test quantum-geometric electrostatic bounds. The experiment uses the room-temperature IXS spectra of diamond and LiF described for the quantum-Fisher-information measurement, with elastic scattering removed and spectra scaled by the f-sum rule using the finite 100 eV electronic window and the ω^-3 high-energy extrapolation. The real part χ'(q,ω) is computed from χ''(q,ω) using the Kramers-Kronig relation χ'(q,ω)=(2/π)P∫_0^∞ dω' [ω'χ''(q,ω')/(ω'^2-ω^2)], where P denotes the principal value. The static electronic dielectric function is then obtained from ε(q,ω)^-1 = 1 + V(q)χ(q,ω), with V(q)=4πe^2/q^2, evaluated at a small nonzero energy inside the electronic gap; the figure caption specifies extraction below the gap at 4.5 eV. Momentum transfers are along (1,-1,0) for diamond and (1,0,0) for LiF, with momentum resolution about 0.15 Å^-1. The high-q spatial-frequency regime is analyzed by fitting the extracted ε(q) for q approximately greater than 2.5 Å^-1 to the short-wavelength screening form ε(q)=1+exp(-αq), where q is in Å^-1 and α is the fitted real-space decay length in Å associated with atomic-scale electronic polarization. The beam footprint, number of real-space locations, and independent sample count are not specified; homogeneity is assessed only through the reciprocal-space q-dependent dielectric response extracted from the spectra.

Experiment 3

Central concept: the IXS-derived Bures metric is converted into the dimensionless longitudinal quantum weight aK(q), a reciprocal-space measure of electronic localization and quantum-information sharing in insulating bonds, to compare a covalent insulator, diamond, against an ionic insulator, LiF. The analysis uses the same room-temperature IXS density-response spectra, elastic-line removal, f-sum normalization, ω^-3 extrapolation above 100 eV, and q directions as in the QFI measurement: diamond at 8981.8 eV with Ge(733) analyzer, 0.3 eV energy resolution, q parallel to (1,-1,0); LiF at 6482.5 eV with Ge(531) analyzer, 140 meV energy resolution, q parallel to (1,0,0); momentum resolution about 0.15 Å^-1. The measured q range used for the quantum-weight plot is the finite-q electronic-response range shown in the paper, approximately q≈1.5–4.6 Å^-1 for diamond and q≈1.3–3.8 Å^-1 for LiF. The quantity compared is aK(q)=-(πa/2q^2)f_Q, equivalently proportional to a g_q/q^2, where a is the primitive lattice constant, q is the magnitude of the momentum transfer, f_Q is the quantum Fisher information density, and g_q is the Bures metric obtained from χ''(q,ω). The interpretation is that aK(q) below or above unity in the small-q limit distinguishes charge fluctuations largely confined within one primitive unit cell from fluctuations extending over multiple primitive unit cells. The real-space beam footprint, scan area, number of sample locations, and defect/edge exclusion criteria are not specified; the reported homogeneity context is the reproducibility of the q-dependent reciprocal-space trend after applying the stated spectral normalization and elastic removal.

Experiment 4

Central concept: the experimentally extracted dimensionless quantum weight aK(q) is tested against wavevector-dependent electrostatic bounds that constrain the possible quantum geometry of density fluctuations in an insulator. The test uses room-temperature IXS spectra of diamond and LiF scaled by the f-sum rule, extrapolated above Ω=100 eV as ω^-3, converted to χ''(q,ω), integrated to obtain f_Q and g_q, and combined with ε(q) extracted from the Kramers-Kronig procedure. Diamond is measured with q parallel to (1,-1,0), incident energy 8981.8 eV, Ge(733) analyzer, and 0.3 eV energy resolution; LiF is measured with q parallel to (1,0,0), incident energy 6482.5 eV, Ge(531) analyzer, and 140 meV energy resolution; both use momentum resolution about 0.15 Å^-1 and room-temperature electronic energy-loss scans up to 100 eV. The bounds evaluated are (1-ε(q)^-1)E_g ≤ 4e^2K(q) ≤ sqrt(1-ε(q)^-1)ℏω_p, with the dimensionless plotted quantity multiplied by the primitive lattice constant a. Here ε(q) is the electronic static longitudinal dielectric function extracted from IXS, E_g is the smallest electronic excitation gap used for the material, ω_p=sqrt(4πne^2/m) is the plasma frequency defined using the effective electron density n from the partial f-sum rule, e is the elementary charge, m is the bare electron mass, and K(q) is the longitudinal quantum weight derived from the Bures metric. The q-dependent comparison is made over the same finite momentum ranges as the aK(q) extraction. The spatial/geometric scope is reciprocal-space only; beam footprint, real-space scan area, and number of independently measured sample positions are not specified.

First Experimental Demonstration of Beam Storage by Three-Dimensional Spiral Injection Scheme for Ultra-Compact Storage Rings

2602.01504
Prediction Quality0.9399
Numeric rel. CRPS0.2260
Coverage @1σ0.6250
Coverage @2σ0.9375
Bool Brier0.0082
Categorical Briern/a
Bool/Categorical Accuracy1.0000
Formula Accuracyn/a

Experiment 1

A proof-of-principle ultra-compact weak-focusing electron storage ring is operated to test the central concept of three-dimensional spiral injection: an injected beam enters a solenoid-type storage magnet with a vertical pitch angle so that the solenoidal fringe field and a weak-focusing radial field guide the orbit in three dimensions, while a comparatively long pulsed kicker supplies small vertical kicks over many turns rather than requiring a single-turn nanosecond kicker. The electron beam is generated by a thermionic electron gun and chopper system with momentum p = 297 keV/c, kinetic energy 80 keV, and injected bunch width 100 ns. The transport line consists of three rotatable quadrupole magnets, one bending magnet, and a pair of steering magnets; it injects the beam into a 22 cm storage ring inside a storage magnet at a pitch angle of 0.7 rad relative to the horizontal X-Y plane. The storage magnet consists of main and auxiliary solenoidal coils operated with opposite polarities to keep the solenoidal field on the storage midplane Z = 0 cm at Bz = 8.77 mT while producing a weak-focusing field potential with field index of order 10^-2 in the storage region. A kicker coil system with inductance 1.3 microhenry is located at Z = ±15 cm and is driven, for the kicker-on condition, with a 45 A, 140 ns pulsed current to generate a radial field component configured to maintain downward steering during spiral injection. The beam revolution period in the compact ring is 4.7 ns. Beam storage is diagnosed destructively with a plastic scintillating-fiber probe inserted vertically from above into the storage region; the fiber is 1 mm in diameter and 20 cm long, read by a photomultiplier tube, and has an electron CSDA range of about 100 micrometre for 297 keV/c electrons in the scintillator material. For the representative storage test, the probe tip is positioned at the storage midplane so that the fiber covers Z = 0 cm to Z = 20 cm within the storage region. The SciFi-probe voltage waveform, a bottom plastic-scintillator beam-loss monitor waveform, and the kicker current waveform measured by a Rogowski coil are recorded for kicker-on and kicker-off conditions, with each waveform averaged over 1000 injected shots. The operational storage criterion is defined as a SciFi-probe signal exceeding 5 times sigma_noise for a duration of at least 1 microsecond, where sigma_noise is the rms fluctuation of the voltage in a pre-injection time window; this threshold corresponds to a time much longer than the injected pulse width and to many revolutions, and is intended to distinguish stored beam from transiently circulating or lost particles.

Experiment 2

The vertical distribution of the stored beam is measured to test whether the beam accumulated by the three-dimensional spiral injection method is governed by the intentionally applied weak-focusing field rather than by accidental trapping. The same 297 keV/c, 80 keV, 100 ns pulsed electron beam, 0.7 rad pitch-angle injection, 22 cm ultra-compact weak-focusing storage ring, Bz = 8.77 mT storage-plane solenoidal field, and 45 A, 140 ns kicker-pulse spiral-injection configuration are used. Four weak-focusing field configurations are generated by setting the main and auxiliary solenoid coil currents to (IMAIN, IAUX) = (8.70 A, 5.20 A), (8.82 A, 5.67 A), (9.20 A, 7.16 A), and (9.60 A, 8.72 A), with opposite current polarities in the actual magnet circuit. For each configuration, the radial magnetic-field component Br as a function of vertical coordinate Z is characterized in the storage region and used to determine the storable vertical range; finite-element calculations are performed with Opera for comparison, and Monte Carlo tracking includes the dominant injection-trajectory uncertainty. The stored-beam population is probed with the same vertically inserted plastic scintillating-fiber detector, 1 mm in diameter and 20 cm long, read out by a photomultiplier tube. A Z-scan is performed by inserting the probe from above in 5 mm steps; at each probe-tip position Z, measured from the storage midplane Z = 0 cm, a waveform averaged over 1000 injected shots is recorded. The measured dependent signal for the scan is the time-integrated SciFi-probe voltage ISignal = integral VSignal(t) dt, where VSignal(t) is the measured probe voltage and the integration window is t = 560 ns to 9000 ns after injection, beginning just after the end of the kicker pulse. The upper edge of the stored-beam distribution is defined as the probe-tip Z position where ISignal decreases to 5 percent of its maximum value for that field configuration. This spatially resolved scan covers the vertical storage aperture region accessible from above and uses the integrated signal to average over a wide range of vertical and radial betatron phases.

Experimental characterization of the hierarchy of quantum correlations in top quark pairs

Experiment 1

Central concept and measurement configuration: use proton-proton collisions recorded with the CMS detector at the CERN LHC at center-of-mass energy sqrt(s)=13 TeV to treat the spin-1/2 top quark and top antiquark in top-quark-pair production as a two-qubit quantum system. The analysis uses the published CMS doubly differential lepton+jets measurement of top-quark and antiquark polarization coefficients and spin-correlation coefficients in bins of the top-pair invariant mass m(ttbar) and the absolute top production angle |cos(theta)|, where theta is defined in the ttbar center-of-mass frame with respect to the proton-beam direction. The lepton+jets topology consists of one W boson decaying leptonically and the other hadronically; the spin analyzers are the charged lepton and the down-type quark from the W decay. The spatial-geometric analogue for this collider measurement is the resolved two-dimensional phase-space scope: m(ttbar) bins [300,400], [400,600], [600,800], and [800,13000] GeV crossed with |cos(theta)| bins [0.0,0.4], [0.4,0.7], and [0.7,1.0]. The spin basis is the helicity basis, an event-dependent right-handed coordinate system in the ttbar rest frame with unit vectors (khat, nhat, rhat), where khat is the top-quark flight direction, rhat=(phat-cos(theta) khat)/sin(theta), nhat=rhat x khat, phat is the proton-beam direction, and the sign convention is nhat -> sgn(cos(theta)) nhat, rhat -> sgn(cos(theta)) rhat, khat -> khat. Because this basis changes orientation with event kinematics, the derived spin states correspond to the event-dependent, or fictitious, quantum-state construction used to characterize the hierarchy of quantum correlations. In each phase-space bin reconstruct the two-qubit spin density matrix rho_ttbar = (1/4)[I2 tensor I2 + sum_i P_i sigma_i tensor I2 + sum_j Pbar_j I2 tensor sigma_j + sum_ij C_ij sigma_i tensor sigma_j], where I2 is the 2 by 2 identity, sigma_i are Pauli matrices, P_i and Pbar_j are the top and antitop polarization components, and C_ij is the 3 by 3 spin-correlation matrix. Evaluate: top-quark discord D_t = S(rho_antitop) - S(rho_ttbar) + min_uhat[p_+ S(rho_top,+uhat)+p_- S(rho_top,-uhat)], where S(rho)=-Tr(rho log2 rho), uhat is the projective spin-measurement axis on the antitop, p_+ and p_- are the corresponding outcome probabilities, and rho_top,+uhat and rho_top,-uhat are the post-measurement top-quark states; the entanglement marker Delta_E from the Peres-Horodecki criterion as reported for the same reconstructed density matrices, with Delta_E greater than 1 defining the entangled region; the steering marker T = integral d^2 uhat sqrt(uhat^T C^T C uhat), with the integration over all unit-vector orientations and threshold 2*pi; the Bell-correlation marker B=m1+m2, where m1 and m2 are the two largest eigenvalues of C^T C and threshold is 1 for CHSH Bell-correlated states; and the mixed-state quantum magic M2_tilde = -log2([1+sum_i(P_i^4+Pbar_i^4)+sum_ij C_ij^4]/[1+sum_i(P_i^2+Pbar_i^2)+sum_ij C_ij^2]). For each observable, use the Gaussian likelihood -2 log L = sum_ij (o_i-x_i) U_ij^{-1} (o_j-x_j), where o_i are the measured polarization and spin-correlation coefficients in the specified bin, U_ij is their covariance matrix, and x_i are the fitted coefficients constrained to the physical interval [-1,1]; central observable values are obtained at the likelihood minimum while holding the relevant observable definition fixed.

Experiment 2

Central concept and measurement configuration: use proton-proton collisions recorded with the CMS detector at the CERN LHC at center-of-mass energy sqrt(s)=13 TeV to treat the spin-1/2 top quark and top antiquark in top-quark-pair production as a two-qubit quantum system, and evaluate the hierarchy of quantum correlations in a fixed spin basis that corresponds to bona fide spin quantum states. The analysis uses the published CMS doubly differential lepton+jets measurement of top-quark and antiquark polarization coefficients and spin-correlation coefficients in bins of the top-pair invariant mass m(ttbar) and the absolute top production angle |cos(theta)|, where theta is defined in the ttbar center-of-mass frame with respect to the proton-beam direction. The lepton+jets topology consists of one W boson decaying leptonically and the other hadronically; the spin analyzers are the charged lepton and the down-type quark from the W decay. The spatial-geometric analogue for this collider measurement is the resolved two-dimensional phase-space scope: m(ttbar) bins [300,400], [400,600], [600,800], and [800,13000] GeV crossed with |cos(theta)| bins [0.0,0.4], [0.4,0.7], and [0.7,1.0]. The spin basis is the beam basis, a fixed right-handed coordinate system in the ttbar rest frame with axes (xhat,yhat,zhat), where xhat points toward the center of the LHC ring, yhat is vertically upward, and zhat is along the proton beam axis phat. In each phase-space bin reconstruct the two-qubit spin density matrix rho_ttbar = (1/4)[I2 tensor I2 + sum_i P_i sigma_i tensor I2 + sum_j Pbar_j I2 tensor sigma_j + sum_ij C_ij sigma_i tensor sigma_j], where I2 is the 2 by 2 identity, sigma_i are Pauli matrices, P_i and Pbar_j are the top and antitop polarization components, and C_ij is the 3 by 3 spin-correlation matrix. Evaluate: top-quark discord D_t = S(rho_antitop) - S(rho_ttbar) + min_uhat[p_+ S(rho_top,+uhat)+p_- S(rho_top,-uhat)], where S(rho)=-Tr(rho log2 rho), uhat is the projective spin-measurement axis on the antitop, p_+ and p_- are the corresponding outcome probabilities, and rho_top,+uhat and rho_top,-uhat are the post-measurement top-quark states; the entanglement marker Delta_E from the Peres-Horodecki criterion as reported for the same reconstructed density matrices, with Delta_E greater than 1 defining the entangled region; the steering marker T = integral d^2 uhat sqrt(uhat^T C^T C uhat), with the integration over all unit-vector orientations and threshold 2*pi; the Bell-correlation marker B=m1+m2, where m1 and m2 are the two largest eigenvalues of C^T C and threshold is 1 for CHSH Bell-correlated states; and the mixed-state quantum magic M2_tilde = -log2([1+sum_i(P_i^4+Pbar_i^4)+sum_ij C_ij^4]/[1+sum_i(P_i^2+Pbar_i^2)+sum_ij C_ij^2]). For each observable, use the Gaussian likelihood -2 log L = sum_ij (o_i-x_i) U_ij^{-1} (o_j-x_j), where o_i are the measured polarization and spin-correlation coefficients in the specified bin, U_ij is their covariance matrix, and x_i are the fitted coefficients constrained to the physical interval [-1,1]; central observable values are obtained at the likelihood minimum while holding the relevant observable definition fixed.

Energy-resolved transport of ultracold atoms across the Anderson transition: theory and experiment

2602.22063

Experiment 1

Central concept: a three-dimensional, state-dependent optical speckle potential provides the disordered medium in which radio-frequency-selected ultracold 87Rb matter waves are tested for Anderson localization, diffusion, and critical transport. To characterize the disorder used for both the experiment and the ab initio simulations, generate or measure a blue-detuned laser speckle potential with strictly positive potential values. In the numerical implementation, use a Fourier-filtering construction of the speckle electric field on a regular rectangular grid covering a volume of (100 µm)^3, with grid spacings typically Δx = 0.7 µm and Δy = Δz = 0.3 µm. Model the experimental optical system as a λ = 780 nm laser beam propagating along x, linearly polarized in the y–z plane, incident on a diffusive lens with Gaussian beam waist w = 9.9 mm, truncated by a diaphragm of diameter D = 20.4 mm, and focused by a lens of focal length d = 15.2 mm. Set the disorder amplitude to VR/h = 416 Hz. Compute the potential V(r) = VR |E(r)|^2/I from the filtered random complex Gaussian field, and evaluate the normalized autocorrelation C(Δr) = δV(r)δV(r+Δr)/VR^2 with δV = V − VR. The spatial characterization compares two-dimensional cuts through the three-dimensional potential over the simulated 100 µm scale and autocorrelation line cuts along the longitudinal x direction and a transverse direction over displacement ranges sufficient to resolve the speckle-grain half-widths. Correlation lengths are defined as the autocorrelation half width at half maximum divided by 1.39156, and the geometric-mean correlation length σ defines Eσ = ℏ^2/(mσ^2). The goal is to determine the spatial correlation statistics and probability distribution of the disorder potential used for energy-resolved Anderson-transport measurements.

Experiment 2

Central concept: energy-resolved transport across the three-dimensional Anderson transition is enabled by transferring a small fraction of atoms from a disorder-insensitive hyperfine state into a disorder-sensitive state with a radio-frequency pulse whose detuning selects the target energy. Start with an ultracold 87Rb Bose gas in state |1⟩ = |F = 2, mF = 1⟩ in a nearly isotropic optical trap with trap frequencies approximately 2π × (35, 25, 11) Hz, temperature about 7 nK, chemical potential µin/h about 350 Hz, and Thomas–Fermi radii about (8, 12, 26) µm. The atom number is about 2 × 10^5, with a condensate fraction fc used in the energy-distribution model and a residual thermal fraction 1 − fc. Apply a bichromatic state-dependent optical speckle disorder of amplitude VR/h = 416 Hz, and use a weak-coupling radio-frequency pulse of duration trf ≃ 40 ms and Kaiser temporal envelope to transfer approximately 5% of atoms to |2⟩ = |F = 1, mF = −1⟩. The radio-frequency detuning δrf from the bare resonance sets Ef = ℏδrf. Immediately after transfer, switch off the optical trap and remove atoms left in |1⟩. For the formula result, E is the final atom energy in the disorder-sensitive state; Ef is the rf-selected target energy; fc is the condensate fraction; F(E − Ef) is the energy-filter intensity set by the rf pulse envelope; A(E,k=0) is the zero-momentum spectral function in the random potential; kB is Boltzmann’s constant; T is the initial cloud temperature; Θ is the Heaviside step function; and D(E;Ef) is normalized as the transferred-atom energy probability density. The goal is to determine the energy distribution produced by this rf-transfer preparation, including the contribution of the thermal component.

Experiment 3

Central concept: after rf preparation of a narrow-energy matter wave in a three-dimensional disordered potential, the subsequent in-situ density evolution is used to identify localized, critical, and diffusive transport across the Anderson mobility edge. Prepare 87Rb atoms as a Bose-condensed cloud in |1⟩ = |F = 2, mF = 1⟩, transfer about 5% to |2⟩ = |F = 1, mF = −1⟩ using the 40 ms rf pulse in the state-dependent speckle potential of amplitude VR/h = 416 Hz, and choose target energies Ef/h = 166 Hz, 246 Hz, and 366 Hz. After transfer, set t = 0 by switching off the optical trap and removing atoms in |1⟩. Keep the atoms suspended against gravity by magnetic levitation; the residual confinement is shallow and lies in the horizontal x–y plane, while the analysis focuses on expansion along vertical z. Image the atoms in situ by fluorescence along x, giving a column density ncol(y,z,t) = ∫ dx nexp(r,t), then integrate over y to obtain n1d(z,t) = ∫ dy ncol(y,z,t). Record initial profiles immediately after transfer and subsequent profiles at evolution times including 1 s, 3 s, and 5 s. Analyze spatial profiles over the resolved z range shown in the data, approximately from −100 µm to +100 µm for the long-time comparison and about −80 µm to +80 µm for the time-series comparison, with each plotted point averaged over 6–9 independent experimental runs and error bars given as the standard error of the mean. Compare the profiles with the self-consistent-theory prediction using the experimentally measured initial n1d(z,0), an asymmetric pseudo-Voigt fit to that initial profile, and the rf-transfer energy distribution including the measured thermal fraction. The self-consistent-theory fit uses the central density n1d(0,t) over target energies near the transition and times up to 3 s to determine α0 and β, where α0 has units s^(−1/3), β has units µm s^(−1/3), and Ec is the mobility edge separating localized from diffusive dynamics.

Analog-Digital Quantum Computing with Quantum Annealing Processors

2603.15534
Prediction Quality0.6771
Numeric rel. CRPS0.3140
Coverage @1σ0.2917
Coverage @2σ0.5000
Bool Briern/a
Categorical Brier0.0294
Bool/Categorical Accuracy1.0000
Formula Accuracy0.0000

Experiment 1

Central concept tested: a superconducting quantum annealing processor is operated as an analog-digital quantum computer by using independent “multicolor” annealing lines so that auxiliary source qubits implement programmable single-qubit state preparation, target qubits evolve under a time-independent analog Hamiltonian, and auxiliary detector qubits implement programmable-basis readout. The single-qubit characterization uses independent three-qubit source-target-detector systems on a 1178-qubit D-Wave Advantage2-type quantum annealing processor with six low-frequency annealing lines, each line controlling an anneal parameter s. For each source-target-detector triplet, all qubits start at s=0; the source qubit is forward-annealed to s=1 in a polarizing flux bias and the bias is then removed; the target qubit is brought to a fixed operating point with transverse splitting Delta/h=1.0 GHz and no programmed longitudinal field; the source is rapidly quenched back to s=0 to define the target initial state; after a variable free-evolution time t under H0=-(Delta/2) sigma_z, the detector qubit is rapidly annealed to s=1 and its flux state is measured. Source and detector static flux biases are independently swept from -200 microPhi0 to +200 microPhi0 to map the prepared Bloch vector n_s=(sin(theta_s)cos(phi_s), sin(theta_s)sin(phi_s), cos(theta_s)) and the detector measurement vector n_d=(sin(theta_d)cos(phi_d), sin(theta_d)sin(phi_d), cos(theta_d)). Magnetization data are taken as 1000-shot QPU calls for each t; short-time overlap between initialization and detection is excluded, and fits use the Larmor-precession/decoherence model over the stated fitting window. The fitted observable is <sigma_{n_d}>, where sigma_{n_d}=R_{theta_d,phi_d} sigma_z R^{-1}_{theta_d,phi_d}. The model used for extraction is <sigma_{n_d}>=cos(theta_d)[1-exp(-(t-t0)/T1)(1-cos(theta_s))]+sin(theta_d)sin(theta_s)cos[Delta(t-t0)/hbar+phi_s-phi_d]exp(-(t-t0)/T2), with T2=(1/(2T1)+1/T_phi)^(-1); t0 is the initialization time, T1 is the relaxation time, T_phi is the pure dephasing time, and hbar is the reduced Planck constant. The spatial/statistical scope is the median over 124 independent source-target-detector triplets distributed on the processor, after calibration of per-line delays, per-qubit anneal offsets, and per-qubit flux biases to align the degeneracy point and Larmor phase.

Experiment 2

Central concept tested: the same multicolor analog-digital annealing protocol should remain usable when target-target couplings are left on during initialization and readout, so that two-qubit analog evolution can be measured in more than one basis. The experiment uses pairs of target qubits q1 and q2 on the 1178-qubit superconducting annealing processor, each target having a distinct source qubit and a distinct detector qubit controlled by separate annealing-line classes. All target qubits are operated at Delta/h=1.0 GHz with site detunings deltaDelta_i=0 and no programmed longitudinal fields. The two-target effective Hamiltonian in the weak-coupling rotating-wave regime is H_2Q=(J/4)(sigma_x1 sigma_x2+sigma_y1 sigma_y2), where J is the programmed exchange coupling energy. The protocol prepares product states using source-qubit pi/2 or pi pulses, evolves for variable time t over the plotted 0-30 ns window, and reads either sigma_x-basis or sigma_z-basis magnetizations using detector-qubit bias settings. Measurements include configurations with J/h=0, J/h=0.30 GHz, and J/h=0.15 GHz in the main characterization, with supplementary sigma_z-basis fits also varying J/h over 0.09, 0.15, 0.30, and 0.60 GHz. Magnetizations and the correlator <sigma_z1 sigma_z2> are averaged over 2000 shots for each time point in the main figure; for the fitted sigma_z exchange data, two 1000-shot calls with opposite source polarizing biases are combined to suppress state-preparation and measurement bias. The relaxation of <sigma_z1 sigma_z2> and the dephasing of the oscillatory difference <sigma_z1>-<sigma_z2> are fit to an independent-noise two-qubit Lindblad model with identical per-qubit T1 and T_phi, using expressions such as <sigma_z1>=1-exp(-t/T1)[1+exp(-t/T_phi)cos(Jt/hbar)], <sigma_z2>=1-exp(-t/T1)[1-exp(-t/T_phi)cos(Jt/hbar)], and <sigma_z1 sigma_z2>=1-2exp(-t/T1). The quoted fitted medians are obtained from 62 different target-qubit pairs for the sigma_z-basis exchange condition with J/h=0.30 GHz.

Experiment 3

Central concept tested: after source-qubit preparation and detector-qubit readout, a large chain of target qubits in a quantum annealer can be used as an analog simulator of coherent many-body dynamics; in the weak-coupling regime the target Hamiltonian is described by an effective XY model or, equivalently for the clean periodic chain, by a transverse-field Ising model whose single-excitation dispersion can be extracted from space-time-resolved magnetization. The experiment embeds a periodic one-dimensional chain of L=56 target qubits on the 1178-qubit processor, with each target coupled to a distinct source and detector qubit; four annealing-line color classes are assigned to target qubits and the remaining two to source and detector qubits. The target chain is programmed with uniform transverse splitting Delta/h=2.0 GHz, zero site detuning deltaDelta_i=0, nearest-neighbor periodic exchange coupling J/h=-0.60 GHz, and no longitudinal fields. A single target site is prepared in |+> by a source-qubit pi/2 operation while the rest of the chain is left in the target ground-state basis, the chain evolves for variable detector delay corresponding to normalized time |J|t/hbar spanning the plotted range, and all targets are read out in the sigma_x basis by detector qubits. The spatial scope is the full 56-site periodic ring; magnetization maps are averaged over all possible locations of the initially excited qubit and over the chain’s site-order-reversal symmetry, with each time/location measurement obtained from QPU shot averages. A two-dimensional Fourier transform over target-site index and evolution time is used to extract the magnetization spectral ridge. In the formula result, k is the quasimomentum in the first Brillouin zone [-pi,pi], Delta is the target transverse energy splitting, J is the nearest-neighbor exchange energy, E(k) is the positive or negative single-particle excitation energy, h is Planck’s constant, and hbar=h/(2pi).

Experiment 4

Central concept tested: the multicolor analog-digital protocol on a quantum annealer should measure a different dynamical correlator when the same clean target-chain analog evolution is initialized and read out in the energy basis rather than the transverse basis, allowing extraction of the energy-difference spectral structure of a periodic spin chain. The experiment embeds a periodic one-dimensional chain of L=124 target qubits on the 1178-qubit processor, with each target having a distinct source and detector qubit. Four annealing-line color classes control the target chain and two separate line classes control source and detector qubits. The uniform target parameters are Delta/h=2.0 GHz, nearest-neighbor periodic exchange coupling J/h=-0.60 GHz, zero site detuning deltaDelta_i=0, and no programmed longitudinal fields. A single target site is prepared in |1> by a source-qubit pi pulse, the target chain evolves under the fixed clean-chain Hamiltonian for detector delays spanning the plotted normalized-time range |J|t/hbar, and all targets are measured in the sigma_z basis using detector-qubit readout. The spatial scope is the complete 124-site periodic ring; magnetization-derived excitation probabilities are averaged over all initially excited locations and over the chain’s bracelet symmetry, with QPU shot averaging for each time point. The measured field analyzed is the excitation probability rho(n,t)=(1-<sigma_z(n,t)>)/2, and a two-dimensional Fourier transform over site n and time t is used to locate the intensity ridge. In the formula result, k is quasimomentum in [-pi,pi], J is the nearest-neighbor exchange energy, h is Planck’s constant, and f(k) is the frequency of the Fourier-transform intensity peak.

Experiment 5

Central concept tested: the analog-digital quantum annealing protocol can intentionally program site-dependent target-qubit detunings in an effective XY chain and use arbitrary-basis initialization/readout to probe disorder-induced Anderson localization through the persistence of a spin-density-wave imbalance. The experiment uses a periodic one-dimensional chain of L=124 target qubits on the 1178-qubit processor, with each target assigned a distinct source and detector qubit. The clean target parameters are transverse splitting Delta/h=2.0 GHz, nearest-neighbor exchange coupling J/h=0.20 GHz, and no programmed longitudinal field. Odd-indexed target qubits are prepared in |1> by source-qubit pi pulses to create the staggered initial configuration |1010...>, while even-indexed target qubits are left unexcited; all targets are then evolved under a fixed disordered chain Hamiltonian and measured in the sigma_z basis. Disorder is applied by programming per-qubit anneal offsets that implement detunings deltaDelta_i drawn uniformly from [-J W/2, J W/2], where W is the dimensionless disorder strength; unwanted coupling changes due to the same anneal offsets are compensated by reprogramming Jij values. The measured disorder strengths include W=0, 2, 4, and 8 for time traces over normalized time Jt/hbar spanning the plotted window, with QPU data averaged over 20 independent disorder realizations. The imbalance is computed from excitation probabilities as I(t)=[p_odd(t)-p_even(t)]/[p_odd(t)+p_even(t)], where p_odd=(1/L) sum_{i=1}^{L/2}[1-<sigma_z_{2i-1}>] and p_even=(1/L) sum_{i=1}^{L/2}[1-<sigma_z_{2i}>]. The long-time summary quantity I_tilde is the QPU imbalance averaged over normalized times Jt/hbar from 6*pi to 8*pi, and its small-W dependence is fit to a quadratic scaling form.

Experiment 1

Central concept: xenon strong-field ionization driven by BSV tests whether a nonclassical, zero-mean, strongly fluctuating optical field suppresses phase-sensitive above-threshold-ionization structures while preserving dynamically correlated photoelectron-holography pathways. BSV pulses generated by type-I degenerate SPDC near 1600 nm are focused by a 75 mm focal-length concave mirror into a pulsed supersonic xenon gas jet inside a COLTRIMS reaction microscope held at 5×10^-11 mbar. The xenon backing pressure is 0.7 atm, chosen to provide ionization yield while avoiding space-charge effects. Four BSV average pulse-energy conditions are used: 3.3, 5.0, 8.3, and 10 µJ, corresponding to average peak intensities of 0.53×10^14, 0.80×10^14, 1.33×10^14, and 1.60×10^14 W/cm^2. The BSV photon statistics are the characterized super-Poissonian distributions for these energy conditions. Electrons from the xenon target are extracted and guided by calibrated electric and magnetic fields to a microchannel-plate detector with a delay-line detector, giving time- and position-resolved single-electron detection. Millions of events are accumulated for each optical condition to reconstruct three-dimensional electron momenta. The reported momentum-distribution scope includes two-dimensional projections in p_x versus p_z over approximately p_x = -0.4 to 0.4 a.u. and p_z = -0.6 to 0.6 a.u., and cylindrical projections p_r = sqrt(p_x^2+p_y^2) versus p_z over approximately p_r = 0 to 1 a.u. and p_z = -0.6 to 0.6 a.u. The measurement targets interference features associated with direct, indirect, and forward-scattered subcycle electron trajectories in the focal-volume overlap of the gas jet and BSV beam; no spatially resolved target-volume map is reported.

Experiment 1

Central concept: bright squeezed vacuum (BSV) generated by high-gain spontaneous parametric down-conversion is used as a quantum-light driver for high-harmonic generation, so the source must be characterized in photon statistics and spectral Schmidt-mode content before using it for attosecond quantum spectroscopy. A commercial femtosecond Yb:YAG fiber laser supplies near-infrared pump pulses at 1030 nm, 2 mJ pulse energy, 140 fs duration, and tunable repetition rate from 1 Hz to 10 kHz. A reduced-diameter portion of the pump is sent through two tandem type-I BBO crystals, each 3 mm thick, separated by approximately 40 cm to favor the lowest-divergence spatial mode. The SPDC output is spectrally filtered by a band-pass filter centered at 2050 nm with 100 nm FWHM to target the degenerate Schmidt mode. The single-pulse spectrum of the filtered quantum light is recorded with an AvaSpec-NIR256-2.5-HSC-EVO near-infrared spectrometer. More than 10000 single-shot spectra I(lambda) are analyzed by constructing the intensity covariance Cov(lambda,lambda') = <I(lambda)I(lambda')> - <I(lambda)><I(lambda')>, normalizing it to C(lambda,lambda') = Cov(lambda,lambda') / integral integral Cov(lambda,lambda') dlambda dlambda', and applying singular-value decomposition to extract orthonormal spectral Schmidt modes u_m(lambda) and mode weights lambda_m. Photon-number statistics are evaluated shot by shot; the time-domain zero-delay second-order correlation is defined here as g2_tau0 = <N^2>/<N>^2, where N is the photon number per pulse in the measured filtered BSV mode. The optical path and measurement target the full collected SPDC beam after filtering rather than a selected spatial subregion; the spectral scope covers the near-infrared SPDC band around 1.8-2.3 micrometers, with spectral homogeneity assessed through the Schmidt-mode decomposition of the >10000-shot covariance matrix.

Experiment 2

Central concept: high-harmonic generation in a solid is driven by a spectrally filtered degenerate-mode BSV field to test whether quantum photon-number fluctuations of the infrared driver are transferred to ultraviolet harmonics and whether the harmonic photon statistics can diagnose microscopic intraband-versus-interband emission channels. The quantum-light source is produced by high-gain type-I SPDC in two tandem 3 mm BBO crystals pumped at 1030 nm by 140 fs Yb:YAG pulses, with the SPDC output filtered by a 2050 nm, 100 nm FWHM band-pass filter. The filtered BSV driver is focused by an off-axis parabolic mirror of focal length 25.4 mm into the active region of a 200 micrometer thick free-standing x-cut lithium niobate crystal. The driver polarization is aligned along the crystal Gamma-Z axis. The driving intensity is set in the strong-field, nonperturbative solid-state HHG regime and approaches 1.2 TW/cm2 for the main single-mode measurement. Harmonics emitted from the illuminated through-thickness interaction volume are collected by a matching off-axis parabolic mirror and spectrally resolved with a Princeton Instruments HRS-500 spectrometer equipped with a PIX-100B high-efficiency detector. The measurement records single-pulse-resolved harmonic spectra, and photon-number distributions are evaluated separately for individual harmonic orders in the 2nd through 10th order range. For each harmonic order, N denotes the photon number in the spectral peak assigned to that harmonic order, and the reported photon bunching metric is g2_tau0 = <N^2>/<N>^2. The spectral-geometric scope is the collected emission from the focused spot in the 200 micrometer LN crystal, with harmonic coverage spanning the 2nd-10th orders of the approximately 2060 nm driver; comparison to the lithium niobate field-free bandgap of 3.3 eV provides the physical context for below-gap and above-gap harmonic channels.

Observation of feedback-directed quantum dynamics in large-scale quantum processors

2604.11900
Prediction Quality0.5875
Numeric rel. CRPS1.2374
Coverage @1σ0.2500
Coverage @2σ0.3750
Bool Briern/a
Categorical Briern/a

Formula Accuracyn/a

Experiment 1

Central concept tested: feedback-directed quantum dynamics in random superconducting-qubit circuits, where spatially structured mid-circuit measurements are promoted from passive readout to real-time classical control signals. The experiment compares whether measurement alone or measurement-conditioned feedback creates directional information flow in an otherwise statistically symmetric random circuit, distinct from non-Hermitian skin-effect implementations with built-in asymmetric couplers. The hardware platform is the IBM Quantum superconducting processor “marrakesh” using native single-qubit RX rotations and CZ gates on a 1D chain of L = 20 qubits. Each circuit layer consists of a random scrambling unitary Urand built from alternating even- and odd-bond nearest-neighbor CZ gates plus independent random RX rotations RX_j(theta_j), with theta_j sampled in the strong-scrambling regime used in the main text. The initial computational-basis state contains six occupied qubits localized near the center of the 20-qubit chain. The layer index i is swept from 1 to 10. Four architectures are executed and compared over 10 independent random circuit realizations: (1) a purely unitary random circuit Uunitary(i) = [Urand]^i; (2) a random circuit with pure mid-circuit computational-basis measurements after each random layer, each qubit x measured with probability f(x) = (L - x)g using g = 0.01 and no feedback; (3) a conditional-X feedback circuit in which a measured outcome 0 triggers an X gate on the same qubit, with the same f(x) = (L - x)g and g = 0.01; and (4) a conditional-SWAP feedback circuit in which a measured outcome 0 on qubit x triggers a SWAP between x and x+1 with spatially uniform application probability pSWAP = 0.3. The measured local density is n_x = |0_x><0_x|, and the reported transport observable is the normalized center of mass Nc(i) = [sum_x x <n_x(i)>]/[sum_x <n_x(i)>], plotted in the paper in dimensionless chain-coordinate units. The spatial scope is the full 20-site chain; the local density profiles are resolved at every qubit and every circuit layer, and the center-of-mass values are averaged over the 10 random circuit realizations with standard-deviation error bars.

Experiment 2

Central concept tested: scalability of feedback-directed non-unitary control in large random superconducting-qubit circuits. The experiment quantifies whether conditional-X feedback, driven by a spatially dependent mid-circuit measurement record, produces a center-of-mass displacement on a 100-qubit IBM Quantum chain, and compares it against two controls that lack feedback-directed dynamics. The hardware is the IBM Quantum processor “marrakesh,” with a 100-qubit low-noise 1D chain selected from the device layout. Each layer uses the same random scrambling block Urand composed of alternating nearest-neighbor CZ gates and random RX rotations, followed where applicable by a measurement layer. The initial state contains six occupied qubits. Two spatial placements are tested: six occupied qubits at the chain center and six occupied qubits near the right edge. The layer index i is swept from 1 to 10. The three circuit classes are: a purely unitary random circuit; a random circuit with position-dependent mid-circuit computational-basis measurements only; and a conditional-X feedback circuit in which qubit x is measured with probability f(x) = g(L - x), with g = 0.15/L, and outcome 0 triggers an X gate on that qubit. The measured density operator is n_x = |0_x><0_x|. The reported observable is the net center-of-mass shift delta_Nc(i) = L[Nc(i) - Nc(1)], where Nc(i) = [sum_x x <n_x(i)>]/[sum_x <n_x(i)>] and L = 100. The spatial scope is the full 100-qubit chain; the observable is computed from qubit-resolved final readout over all sites, and the plotted curves compare independent random circuit realizations averaged as in the main-text large-scale measurements.

Experiment 3

Central concept tested: tunability of feedback-directed pumping by the strength of the spatially structured mid-circuit measurement-and-feedback channel. The experiment uses the conditional-X architecture on the IBM Quantum superconducting processor “marrakesh” to determine how the net center-of-mass displacement changes when the measurement gradient is varied. The circuit is a 1D chain of L = 50 qubits initialized with six occupied qubits at the center. Each layer consists of a random scrambling unitary Urand formed from alternating nearest-neighbor CZ gates and random RX rotations, followed by position-dependent computational-basis mid-circuit measurements. Qubit x is measured with probability f(x) = g(L - x), and a measurement outcome 0 triggers a conditional X gate on the same qubit. The gradient g is swept over the plotted values g = 0.002, 0.004, 0.006, and 0.008; circuit depth i is swept to 5 layers. The density operator is n_x = |0_x><0_x|, and the reported observable is delta_Nc(i) = L[Nc(i) - Nc(1)], with Nc(i) = [sum_x x <n_x(i)>]/[sum_x <n_x(i)>] and L = 50. The spatial scope is the full 50-qubit chain, with all qubit positions contributing to the center-of-mass estimate; error bars in the plotted data represent variation over independent random circuit realizations.

Experiment 4

Central concept tested: a boundary-to-boundary polarization diagnostic for detecting feedback-directed transport in large random circuits when local bulk density profiles are noisy or spatially broad. The experiment runs large-scale IBM Quantum simulations on the superconducting processor “marrakesh” for L = 50, 80, and 100 qubit chains. Each chain is initialized with six occupied qubits at the center. The layer index i is swept from 1 to 10. Two circuit classes are compared: a purely unitary random circuit made from alternating nearest-neighbor CZ gates and random RX rotations, and a conditional-X feedback circuit with the same random scrambling layers followed by position-dependent mid-circuit computational-basis measurements. For the feedback circuit, qubit x is measured with probability f(x) = g(L - x), using the size-scaled gradient g = 0.18/L, and outcome 0 triggers an X gate on the measured qubit. The measured operator is n_x = |0_x><0_x|. The reported end-to-end polarization is Jc(i) = [<n_{L-1}(i)> - <n_0(i)>]/[sum_x <n_x(i)>], directly comparing the two ends of the full chain while normalizing by the total measured population. The spatial scope consists of the full chain for normalization and the two endpoint qubits for the numerator; each data set is averaged over 10 random circuit realizations with mean-deviation error bars and fitted solid curves.

Polymer-free van der Waals assembly of 2D material heterostructures using muscovite crystals

2604.12264
Prediction Quality0.9315
Numeric rel. CRPS0.3527

Coverage @2σ0.6923
Bool Brier0.0145
Categorical Briern/a

Formula Accuracyn/a

Experiment 1

Central concept: use thin, crystalline, optically transparent muscovite mica as a polymer-free van der Waals transfer medium whose adhesion to 2D materials is tuned by temperature, enabling deterministic pick-up, stacking, and release without organic polymer contact. Atomic-force-microscopy-grade muscovite crystals are mechanically exfoliated with adhesive tape to produce few-hundred-nanometer-thick mica layers. For membrane stamps, a mica sheet is suspended over a 5–6 mm punched hole in Nitto ELP BT-50E-FR tape or several layers of Scotch Magic tape, transferred onto a rectangular PDMS block on a microscope glass slide, and tensioned by fixing the tape to the glass-slide sides. For cantilever stamps, a triangular mica sheet is cleaved from the tape edge, mounted on a metal support angled about 15 degrees on a microscope glass slide, and used as a freestanding transparent cantilever. Source mica and prepared stamps are stored under inert gas or in a dry vacuum desiccator; transfers are performed in argon glove-box or air conditions with ambient relative humidity kept below about 50% to limit water adsorption. For hBN/graphene/hBN assembly, a Si/SiO2 substrate containing an hBN flake is preheated to 50–90 °C, the mica stamp is brought into contact under an optical microscope by z-axis motion, the contact front is moved by z approach or by changing substrate temperature within ±10 °C, and full contact is made with the target flake before retraction. Subsequent 2D layers such as graphene are picked up using the already collected top hBN. For release, a target substrate carrying a bottom hBN flake is heated to 120–180 °C; the stack is brought into contact with the bottom hBN and the contact front is swept by temperature variation while leaving part of the bottom hBN uncovered by mica to test selective release of only the intended stack. The spatial scope is optical-microscope-guided flake-scale transfer over tens of micrometers in the active heterostructure region, using the mica membrane or cantilever as the only solid transfer support in place of PC/PPC polymer.

ResultDescriptionGround TruthPredictedUncertaintyStatus
polymer_free_hbn_graphene_hbn_heterostructure_formedWhether the mica membrane or cantilever protocol produced the intended hBN/graphene/hBN van der Waals heterostructure without a polymer transfer layer contacting the stack.truetrueP(true) = 93.0%P(true) = 93% (match)
selective_stack_release_onto_bottom_hbn_demonstratedWhether the temperature-controlled mica protocol enabled release of the intended stack onto bottom hBN while retaining unwanted picked-up flakes on the mica stamp.truetrueP(true) = 88.0%P(true) = 88% (match)

Experiment 2

Central concept: evaluate whether the polymer-free mica cantilever transfer leaves atomically flat, clean hBN-encapsulated graphene interfaces without solvent cleaning or annealing. A single-layer graphene flake is fully encapsulated between top and bottom hBN using mica-cantilever-assisted assembly and the standard bottom-hBN release mechanism. The released stack is characterized as-is by contact-mode atomic force microscopy over the working area of the heterostructure, with the roughness extracted from a defined interior analysis window inside a several-micrometer AFM scan, away from visible flake edges and macroscopic contamination pockets. The measurement targets surface height variations of the top hBN over the active graphene-containing region after polymer-free release.

Experiment 3

Central concept: test a polymer-free bottom-up assembly route in which a mica cantilever is incorporated as the supporting substrate by flipping the assembly and mechanically breaking off the cantilever, avoiding reliance on an additional bottom hBN release flake. The sample is examined as-is by contact-mode AFM on the active hBN/graphene/hBN/graphite region, with roughness calculated from an interior analysis box inside a several-micrometer scan, away from flake boundaries and visible defects. The measurement targets whether the mica-supported heterostructure has an atomically flat exposed top surface after the polymer-free break-off release.

Experiment 4

Central concept: determine whether the mica membrane method can assemble a more complex multilayer van der Waals stack with clean interfaces and without post-transfer cleaning. A seven-layer heterostructure is assembled by mica-membrane-assisted transfer with the layer sequence hBN / twisted trilayer graphene / hBN / graphite / hBN; each graphene layer in the twisted trilayer graphene is intentionally misaligned by a high angle of about 20 degrees to decouple the graphene layers. The stack is released and characterized as-is without annealing, solvent immersion, or other cleaning. Contact-mode AFM is performed on the active multilayer region over an area exceeding 100 µm², and topographic roughness is extracted from an interior analysis window away from visible edges and bubbles. The measurement targets the surface flatness of the exposed top hBN above the multilayer graphene/graphite structure.

Experiment 5

Central concept: assess whether mica-assisted assembly can produce a clean, marginally twisted hBN/hBN moiré ferroelectric superlattice over a large flake-scale area, using the atomically flat mica surface and low-temperature release to preserve the twist configuration. A cracked hBN flake is used so that one piece is picked up with a mica membrane and released back onto another part of the same hBN flake at 50 °C, targeting marginal angular alignment. The resulting hBN/hBN stack is characterized by AFM height imaging and Kelvin probe force microscopy under scanning-probe conditions. The KPFM scans target nanoscale electrostatic-potential modulation associated with triangular ferroelectric moiré domains over the top hBN flake, including a large-area scan of the whole flake-scale region and a higher-resolution scan near a localized submicron interfacial bubble to test continuity and local deformation of the domain pattern.

Experiment 6

Experiment 8

Central concept: use polymer-free mica-membrane assembly to expose clean conductive twisted bilayer graphene surfaces for high-resolution conductive AFM, while deterministically setting sub-degree twist angles to tune the moiré period. Marginal-angle twisted bilayer graphene devices are assembled on thick graphite flakes by the mica-membrane method with nominal twist angles of about 0.8°, 0.3°, and 0.15°. Conductive AFM is performed in ambient air at room temperature with relative humidity below 56% using a Bruker Dimension Icon AFM, Nanoscope VII controller, PFTUNA module, and Pt-coated Si CONTV-PT tip of approximate radius 25 nm and spring constant about 0.2 N/m. Scanning is in contact mode at 0.5–1 Hz. The 0.8° scan uses 1 mV sample bias, -0.1 V deflection setpoint, and 256 × 256 pixels; the 0.3° scans use 0.4 mV bias, 0.02 V setpoint, and 512 × 512 or 256 × 256 pixels; the 0.15° scans use 7 mV bias, 0.08 V or 0.05 V setpoint, and 512 × 512 or 256 × 256 pixels. Current maps are first-order flattened. The scans span nanoscale fields of view from tens to hundreds of nanometers and target local tip-sample current variation associated with AA/AB/BA domains and domain walls, with the broader uniformity context assessed across up to micrometer-scale exposed TBG surface.

Experiment 9

The measurement targets ballistic transport signatures, magnetic electron focusing, and mobility as functions of carrier density in the active encapsulated graphene channel.

Experiment 10

The measurement targets ballistic bend resistance and the onset and multiplicity of Landau quantization from the top, middle, and bottom graphene layers.

Experiment 11

Central concept: evaluate whether a heterostructure assembled on a mica cantilever and released by flipping and breaking off the cantilever can function as a high-quality gated graphene Hall-bar device with mica retained as part of the substrate stack. The measurement targets hysteresis, Dirac-peak asymmetry, and whether the electronic mean free path reaches the geometric limit.

Experiment 12

Central concept: use solvent-free mica-assisted transfer to fabricate suspended 2D heterostructure membranes suitable for nanomechanics, avoiding liquid processing that can damage suspended devices. A single-layer graphene/hBN heterostructure is assembled with the same mica-assisted transfer conditions used for supported stacks and suspended over a circular hole etched in a bottom hBN flake on SiO2, forming a membrane with diameter d = 3 µm. The membrane surface is inspected by peak-force microscopy over the suspended region to target surface continuity and cleanliness. Static force-deflection AFM is then performed at the center of the suspended membrane; force is swept up to approximately 800 nN while indentation is recorded up to approximately 50 nm. The force-deflection curve is fit to the standard continuum-mechanics model for a circular membrane, extracting Young's modulus E and pre-tension N0, where E is the in-plane elastic modulus of the suspended heterostructure membrane and N0 is the built-in membrane tension per unit length.

Experiment 13

Central concept: characterize the dynamic nanomechanical response of a solvent-free mica-transferred suspended single-layer graphene/hBN heterostructure membrane. The same circular d = 3 µm SLG/hBN membrane suspended over an hBN cavity is mounted inside an Attocube attoDRY800 optical cryostat on an xy positioning stage and cooled to T = 4 K under high vacuum. A power-modulated blue diode laser with wavelength 450 nm actuates membrane motion, and a HeNe red laser with wavelength 633 nm reads out displacement interferometrically; both laser spots are about 1 µm and incident laser powers are below 1 µW. The response amplitude is processed by a vector network analyzer while sweeping drive frequency over the fundamental-mode resonance region around 23.4–24.2 MHz. The resonance spectrum is fit to a linear damped harmonic-oscillator model to extract the resonance frequency f0 and mechanical quality factor Q.

Experiment 14

Experiment 15

Central concept: test whether mica cantilevers can manipulate non-graphene van der Waals ferroelectrics without polymer residues, using folded 3R-MoS2 as a target structure relevant for mirror-twin-boundary ferroelectric interfaces. A large 3R-MoS2 flake is contacted by a mica cantilever; part of the flake is picked up, folded onto itself, and then delaminated and released by sliding the flake off the cantilever while moving parallel to the substrate surface. The resulting folded and unfolded 3R-MoS2 regions are characterized by AFM over interior areas of each region, away from the fold edge and macroscopic flake boundaries. The measurement targets the topographic roughness of the polymer-free folded interface and the adjacent original region.

Experiment 16

Experiment 18

Central concept: characterize environmental control of mica adhesion, which underlies the temperature-tunable polymer-free transfer mechanism. Adhesion forces between gold-plated mica cantilevers and representative surfaces including graphite, hBN, and SiO2 are measured by AFM force-deflection methods in ambient conditions while varying humidity and substrate temperature. High-humidity conditions are defined as relative humidity above about 60%, and low-humidity transfer-relevant conditions are compared while increasing the substrate temperature above room temperature up to the range in which the transfer protocol operates. The measurement targets the contact adhesion force between mica and the 2D-material or substrate surfaces, particularly the sensitivity of mica-hBN adhesion to adsorbed water and the reduction of adhesion at elevated temperature.

Dynamics of wavepackets and entanglement in many-body kicked rotors under quantum resonance

Experiment 1

Numerical Floquet simulation of the two-rotor kicked-rotor model used to illustrate the paper's symmetry-selection mechanism connecting wavepacket spreading and entanglement. The kicking potential is V(theta_1,theta_2)=k_1 cos(theta_1)+k_2 cos(theta_2)+xi cos(theta_1-theta_2), with k_1=0.1, k_2=0.2, and xi=1. Rotor 1 is set at the principal quantum resonance tau_1=4*pi and rotor 2 at the secondary quantum resonance tau_2=2*pi; hbar=T=1. The initial state is the product momentum eigenstate |p_1=0> tensor |p_2=0>. The bipartition for entanglement is rotor 1 versus rotor 2, and linear entropy is S_lin=1-Tr(rho_1^2). The simulation evaluates <p_1^2(t)> over kick steps shown on a logarithmic time axis up to t=1000, and <p_2^2(t)> and S_lin(t) over kick steps up to t=100. The central concept tested is that the partial π translation of the secondary-resonant rotor makes the rotor-1 effective potential mixed-symmetry, the rotor-2 effective potential antisymmetric, and the inter-rotor coupling antisymmetric, so the measured quantities target hybrid, anti-resonant, and entanglement-oscillatory responses respectively.
