Experiment 2

Numerical Floquet simulation of the same two-rotor kicked-rotor potential V(theta_1,theta_2)=k_1 cos(theta_1)+k_2 cos(theta_2)+xi cos(theta_1-theta_2), now with k_1=2, k_2=3, and xi=0.1. Both rotors are tuned to the secondary quantum resonance tau_1=tau_2=2*pi, with hbar=T=1. The initial state is |p_1=0> tensor |p_2=0>, and the entanglement bipartition is rotor 1 versus rotor 2 with linear entropy S_lin=1-Tr(rho_1^2). The wavepacket variances <p_1^2(t)> and <p_2^2(t)> and the linear entropy are evaluated over integer kick steps up to t=1000. This setup tests the central claim that when both rotors undergo the partial π translation, the single-rotor effective potentials have mixed symmetry while the inter-rotor coupling is symmetric, producing mixed wavepacket spreading but initially quadratic entanglement growth before saturation.

Experiment 3

Both rotors are at the secondary resonance tau_1=tau_2=2*pi, with hbar=T=1. The initial state is |p_1=0> tensor |p_2=0>, the bipartition is rotor 1 versus rotor 2, and S_lin=1-Tr(rho_1^2) is evaluated for kick steps up to t=1000. The setup targets the paper's central mixed-symmetry mechanism by adding an interaction component that is neither purely symmetric nor purely antisymmetric under the partial π translation.

Experiment 6

Robustness numerical simulations for the central resonance-controlled dynamics under weak detuning away from exact quantum resonance. The two-rotor potential is V(theta_1,theta_2)=k_1 cos(theta_1)+k_2 cos(theta_2)+xi cos(theta_1-theta_2), with k_1=2, k_2=3, and xi=0.1. The exact-resonance reference has rotor 1 at tau_1=4*pi and rotor 2 at tau_2=2*pi; detuned simulations use tau_1=4*pi+delta_tau_1 and tau_2=2*pi+delta_tau_2, where the offsets are incommensurate with 4*pi. The initial state is |p_1=0> tensor |p_2=0>, hbar=T=1, and linear entropy is computed for the rotor-1/rotor-2 bipartition. For the direct comparison, delta_tau_1=delta_tau_2=10^-3 and <p_1^2(t)>, <p_2^2(t)>, and S_lin(t) are compared to the exact-resonance trajectories up to t=100. For the scaling analysis, delta_tau_1=delta_tau_2=delta_tau is swept over 10^-3, 5*10^-4, 10^-4, 5*10^-5, and 10^-5. The deviation measure is Delta_1=|<p_1^2>_0-<p_1^2>|/<p_1^2>_0, where <p_1^2>_0 is the exact-resonance value. The agreement time t_D is defined as the kick number at which Delta_1 reaches a 1% threshold.

Experiment 7

Numerical simulation of the many-body kicked-top counterpart introduced to test whether the kicked-rotor central mechanism transfers to finite spherical phase space. The model contains two quantum tops with total angular momentum quantum number j_tot=50. The kick operator is U_k=exp[-i sum_{n=1}^2 beta_n J_{nz}^2/(2*j_tot)], and the between-kick Hamiltonian is H_f=alpha_1 J_{1x}+alpha_2 J_{2x}+(alpha_12/j_tot) J_{1x}J_{2x}+(alpha_122/j_tot^2) J_{1x}J_{2x}^2. The parameters are alpha_1=0.0001, alpha_2=0.02, alpha_12=0.005, and alpha_122=0.0005. Top 1 is at the principal kicked-top resonance beta_1=4*pi*j_tot, and top 2 is at the secondary resonance beta_2=2*pi*j_tot. The initial state is |J_{1z}=0> tensor |J_{2z}=0>. The bipartition is top 1 versus top 2, and linear entropy is S_lin=1-Tr(rho_1^2). The simulation tracks the normalized variances <J_{1z}^2>/j_tot^2 and <J_{2z}^2>/j_tot^2 and S_lin over kick numbers up to about 10^4. The setup targets the kicked-top analogue of the mixed-symmetry resonance mechanism, while accounting for the finite spherical phase-space bound on wavepacket spreading.

Wave–particle transition and quantum Zeno effect in which-way experiments with a superconducting quantum processor

2604.19115
Prediction Quality0.8204
Numeric rel. CRPS0.8416

Coverage @2σ0.4167
Bool Brier0.0036
Categorical Brier0.0497
Bool/Categorical Accuracy1.0000
Formula Accuracyn/a

Experiment 1

Four-qubit superconducting-qubit Mach–Zehnder interferometry experiment configured to test a tunable wave-to-particle transition under pulsed which-way measurement. The active interferometer is the Q0–Q1–Q2–Q3 plaquette of a 4×4 frequency-tunable transmon array: Q0 acts as the first beam splitter, Q1 is path 1, Q2 is path 2 and the monitored which-way site, and Q3 acts as the second beam splitter and output port. All four qubits are initially measured and post-selected in |0>. A microwave photon is generated by applying a π pulse to Q0. Q0, Q1, and Q2 are tuned by Z pulses to the common working frequency 5.82 GHz for 57 ns to split the excitation between Q1 and Q2. The arms are then decoupled by biasing Q0 to its idle frequency, keeping Q2 at 5.82 GHz, and biasing Q1 to the waiting frequency 5.83 GHz. A 100 ns dispersive readout pulse is applied to Q2’s readout resonator as the which-way measurement, followed by a 100 ns resonator-decay wait. The calibrated average measurement-induced dephasing strength Γm/2π is swept from 0 to 3.695 MHz. A controllable phase is applied on path 1 by biasing Q1 to 5.83 GHz + δ/2π for tp = 100 ns, with δ/2π swept from 0 to 30 MHz so that the phase is phi = -tp δ. Q1, Q2, and Q3 are then tuned to 5.82 GHz for 49 ns to recombine the two paths at Q3. The Q3 excited-state population PI is measured as a function of δ and fit to a sinusoidal interference fringe. Visibility is defined as V = (PImax - PImin)/(PImax + PImin), where PImax and PImin are the fitted maximum and minimum Q3 populations over the δ sweep. The geometric scope is the entire four-qubit interferometer plaquette; the measurement compares the same output port Q3 across the full phase sweep for each calibrated Γm.

Experiment 2

Two-qubit quantum-state tomography experiment on the two arms of the 4-qubit superconducting-qubit Mach–Zehnder interferometer, configured to quantify how pulsed which-way measurement transfers information from the path qubits to the measurement apparatus. The active interferometer region is the same Q0–Q1–Q2–Q3 plaquette, with Q1 and Q2 representing the two arms after the first beam splitter. The preparation and first beamsplitting stage are identical to the 4-qubit interference experiment: all qubits are initially read out and post-selected in |0>, Q0 is excited by a π pulse, and Q0, Q1, and Q2 are tuned to 5.82 GHz for 57 ns. The qubits are then decoupled, and a 100 ns dispersive which-way readout pulse with calibrated average dephasing Γm is applied to Q2’s readout resonator. The Γm/2π sweep covers 0 to 3.695 MHz. Instead of applying the phase-scan and recombination steps, the circuit waits with Q1 and Q2 decoupled and then performs two-qubit tomography on Q1 and Q2. From the reconstructed density matrix rho_Q1Q2, the concurrence is computed using the Wootters definition C = max(0, λ1 - λ2 - λ3 - λ4), where λi are the square roots of the eigenvalues of rho rho_tilde in decreasing order and rho_tilde = (sigma_y ⊗ sigma_y) rho* (sigma_y ⊗ sigma_y). The single-excitation density matrix is rho_s = P rho_Q1Q2 P / Tr(P rho_Q1Q2), with P = |10><10| + |01><01|. The purity is Ps = Tr(rho_s^2), and the von Neumann entropy is Ss = -Tr(rho_s log2 rho_s). The spatial-geometric scope is the two arm qubits Q1 and Q2, measured jointly after the controlled path-splitting and which-way pulse.

Experiment 3

Twelve-qubit superconducting-qubit Mach–Zehnder interferometry experiment configured to test continuous which-way monitoring and quantum-Zeno backaction during coherent propagation. The active interferometer is embedded in the 4×4 transmon processor and uses 12 qubits. Q0 is the initial beam splitter; path 1 is Q1, Q4, Q5, Q7, and Q13; path 2 is Q2, Q8, Q10, Q11, and Q14; Q15 is the final beam splitter/output region. All participating qubits are initially biased to idle frequencies and measured for initial-state post-selection. A microwave photon is generated by exciting Q0. The 12 interferometer qubits are then tuned to the common working frequency 5.82 GHz for coherent evolution over a time window from 0 to 200 ns. The central mechanism tested is that continuous dispersive monitoring of Q2, a site at the entrance to path 2, can both provide which-way information and alter transport by quantum-Zeno reflection when the measurement-induced dephasing rate becomes comparable to or exceeds the Q0–Q2 hopping scale. The which-way measurement pulse on Q2’s readout resonator is started before initial state preparation so that its strength is stabilized when the joint evolution begins, maintained throughout the 12-qubit evolution, and accompanied by a Z-bias compensation for the measurement-induced Stark shift. Γm/2π is set to calibrated values including 0, 5.921, 11.195, and 18.134 MHz. At each evolution time, the qubit populations <n_i> are obtained by simultaneous projective readout of the 12 active sites. The spatial-geometric scope is the complete 12-site interferometer, with population dynamics compared along both arms and at Q15 over the full 0–200 ns evolution window.

Experiment 4

Two-qubit tomography experiment on the two arms of the continuously monitored 12-qubit superconducting-qubit Mach–Zehnder interferometer, configured to quantify quantum-information leakage and its modification by quantum-Zeno backaction. The active device and interferometer geometry are the 12-site Q0-to-Q15 Mach–Zehnder network on the 4×4 transmon processor: path 1 contains Q1, Q4, Q5, Q7, and Q13, while path 2 contains Q2, Q8, Q10, Q11, and Q14. The monitored which-way site is Q2. Initial-state post-selection, Q0 excitation, tuning of all interferometer qubits to 5.82 GHz, and continuous Q2 dispersive measurement are performed as in the 12-qubit population-dynamics experiment. The calibrated continuous measurement-induced dephasing Γm/2π is swept over the plotted range from 0 to about 24 MHz. Two-qubit tomography is performed on Q4 and Q8, one qubit on each arm downstream of the monitored entrance region, at the fixed evolution time corresponding to the maximum Q4–Q8 concurrence in the absence of the which-way measurement. From the reconstructed rho_Q4Q8, concurrence CQ4Q8 is computed using the Wootters definition. The single-excitation subspace density matrix is formed with P = |10><10| + |01><01| as rho_s = P rho_Q4Q8 P / Tr(P rho_Q4Q8), with purity Ps = Tr(rho_s^2) and von Neumann entropy Ss = -Tr(rho_s log2 rho_s). The spatial-geometric scope is the paired downstream arm sites Q4 and Q8, sampled as a function of the continuously monitored Q2 measurement strength.

Enhanced Atom Capture via Multi-Frequency Magneto-Optical Trapping

2604.23221
Prediction Quality0.8801
Numeric rel. CRPS0.4477
Coverage @1σ0.5667
Coverage @2σ0.5667
Bool Brier0.0026
Categorical Briern/a
Bool/Categorical Accuracy1.0000
Formula Accuracyn/a

Experiment 1

Central concept: implement a practical multi-frequency 87Rb magneto-optical trap (MOT) in which additional red-detuned cooling frequencies are confined primarily to an annular beam surrounding the trapped cloud, so that incident thermal atoms can be slowed over a wider Doppler range while minimizing overlap of far-red cooling light with already trapped atoms. The optical-field generation procedure uses a double-passed acousto-optic modulator driven simultaneously with multiple radio-frequency tones; a lens places the retroreflecting mirror in a geometry that recombines the different AOM deflection angles into one spatially overlapped beam, and a tapered amplifier boosts the resulting multi-frequency output. A second AOM partially cancels the large carrier frequency shift so that the optical components lie at the desired detunings relative to the 87Rb D2 F=2 to F'=3 cooling transition. The resulting multi-frequency beam is transformed from an initially Gaussian beam into an annular cooling beam with a pair of axicon lenses, with an upstream iris controlling the central hole. The conventional trapping light is a Gaussian component near the usual MOT detuning, while the additional cooling components are intended to be further red-detuned and present only in the axicon-produced ring beam. Characterization consists of measuring the optical frequency spectrum with a Fabry–Perot interferometric analyzer over the relevant detuning range relative to the F=2 to F'=3 transition, and imaging the transverse cooling-beam intensity profile across the full beam cross-section after the axicon pair. The spatial characterization targets the entire transverse annular beam profile over the MOT-beam scale; the paper reports a normalized cross-section through the profile but does not report averaging over multiple beam locations or repeated assemblies.

single_sided_red_detuned_multifrequency_spectrum_producedWhether the optical generation procedure produced a spectrum with multiple cooling components on the red-detuned side of the 87Rb cooling transition rather than a symmetric phase-modulation spectrum.truetrueP(true) = 96.0%P(true) = 96% (match)
annular_cooling_beam_profile_producedWhether the axicon-lens beam-shaping procedure produced the intended hollow annular transverse cooling-beam geometry for the multi-frequency light.truetrueP(true) = 94.0%P(true) = 94% (match)

Experiment 2

Central concept: map the atom-capture landscape of a dual-frequency version of the 87Rb MOT to distinguish frequency regimes associated with enhanced loading. The apparatus is a six-beam vapor-cell 87Rb magneto-optical trap without additional Zeeman slower, 2D-MOT source, or atomic beam slowing stage. The measurement uses two cooling optical frequencies near the 87Rb D2 F=2 to F'=3 transition and records the number of atoms loaded after a fixed 5 s MOT loading time while sweeping both the fundamental cooler detuning and the frequency difference between the two cooling components. The plotted scan covers fundamental cooler detunings from approximately -15 MHz to -20 MHz relative to the cooling transition and dual-frequency spacings from 0 MHz to about 25 MHz. Atom number is obtained by absorption imaging: after switching off cooling/trapping beams and magnetic field, an approximately 35 microsecond probe pulse resonant with F=2 to F'=3 is applied after an approximately 15 ms fall time; the optical-depth image is fit to a two-dimensional Gaussian and rescaled to account for the cloud extending beyond the imaging-beam field of view, then integrated over the whole MOT cloud. The scan probes the full trapped cloud at the six-beam intersection for each frequency setting; the paper presents an interpolated surface over the swept parameter plane and does not report independent spatial subregions within the cloud.

Experiment 3

Central concept: compare a conventional single-frequency 87Rb MOT with the proposed multi-frequency MOT, where additional red-detuned cooling components are applied in an annular axicon beam to increase capture velocity from a thermal background while avoiding strong overlap with the trapped cloud. The MOT uses three orthogonal pairs of counter-propagating, non-retroreflected beams. The conventional Gaussian trapping beams have diameter about 38 mm, 1/e^2 radius approximately 15 mm, are truncated at about 19 mm radius, and contain cooling light detuned near -20 MHz from the 87Rb D2 F=2 to F'=3 transition plus repumper light resonant with F=1 to F'=2. Typical per-beam powers are about 20 mW in the trapping light and about 18 mW in the repumper. The multi-frequency condition adds an overlapping axicon-generated annular cooling beam containing about five additional further red-detuned optical components spanning approximately -25 MHz to -45 MHz from F=2 to F'=3, with typical cooling power about 45 mW per beam. Anti-Helmholtz coils provide the MOT quadrupole magnetic field, and the axial magnetic-field gradient is swept over the experimentally accessible range up to about 22 G cm^-1. For each magnetic-field-gradient setting and each optical configuration, MOT load time is varied; at the end of loading an approximately 20 ms interval with only single-frequency trapping light is applied before imaging. Absorption imaging uses an approximately 35 microsecond resonant F=2 to F'=3 probe after about 15 ms free fall, and the complete cloud optical-depth projection is fit to a two-dimensional Gaussian, rescaled to the physical cloud extent, and integrated. Each reported loading rate R and steady-state atom number N_ss is extracted from a fit of the whole-cloud atom number versus load time to N = R*tau*(1 - exp(-t/tau)), where N is atom number, R is the MOT loading rate, tau is the characteristic MOT loading time, and t is the MOT load time; a time-axis offset is allowed in the fit to account for shutter timing. The spatial scope is the entire trapped atom cloud at the beam intersection for each load curve; the paper does not report atom-number averages over separate cloud subregions, but each data point comes from a load-time scan under fixed beam and field conditions.

Experiment 4

Central concept: determine the loading performance of the proposed multi-frequency 87Rb MOT at specific magnetic-field-gradient operating points and compare it with the conventional single-frequency MOT using the same six-beam geometry. The trap uses three orthogonal counter-propagating beam pairs; Gaussian trapping light is tuned near -20 MHz from the 87Rb D2 F=2 to F'=3 transition and repumper light is resonant with F=1 to F'=2, while the multi-frequency condition additionally uses an axicon-generated annular cooling beam with several further red-detuned components between roughly -25 MHz and -45 MHz relative to F=2 to F'=3. The Gaussian beams have approximately 38 mm diameter and about 15 mm 1/e^2 radius with truncation near 19 mm radius; typical per-beam powers are about 20 mW trapping, about 18 mW repumping, and about 45 mW in the annular multi-frequency cooling light. At fixed axial magnetic-field gradients, load time is swept, a final approximately 20 ms single-frequency-only interval is applied, and the full MOT cloud is measured by resonant absorption imaging after about 15 ms fall time with an approximately 35 microsecond probe. Whole-cloud atom number is extracted by optical-depth imaging, two-dimensional Gaussian fitting, rescaling to the physical cloud extent, and integration. Loading curves are fit to N = R*tau*(1 - exp(-t/tau)), where N is atom number, R is loading rate, tau is characteristic loading time, and t is load time. The figure-captioned operating points correspond to fixed axial magnetic-field gradients of 12.0 G cm^-1 for one multi-frequency loading-rate fit and 12.9 G cm^-1 for one multi-frequency steady-state atom-number fit; each value represents one complete load-time scan over the full cloud rather than a local-density measurement.

Experiment 5

Central concept: test how the performance advantage of annular multi-frequency cooling depends on the physical beam diameter, using beam truncation as a controlled way to vary the available capture volume and deceleration distance in a 87Rb MOT. The same six-beam vapor-cell MOT geometry is used, with single-frequency Gaussian trapping light and resonant repumping as in a conventional 87Rb MOT, and with the comparison configuration adding the axicon-generated annular multi-frequency cooling light. The axial magnetic-field gradient is held at 12.9 G cm^-1. Adjustable irises are inserted in well-collimated beam regions and centered on the beam axes; only the iris aperture is varied. The quoted beam diameter is the physically truncated beam diameter measured immediately before the chamber, not the Gaussian 1/e^2 waist. The underlying Gaussian trapping beam has about 15 mm 1/e^2 radius and the full untruncated experimental diameter is about 38 mm; the scan reduces the aperture to smaller diameters while keeping the beam profiles otherwise unchanged and without power re-normalization. For each beam diameter and for both single-frequency and multi-frequency configurations, MOT load time is swept and the atom number is obtained by resonant absorption imaging of the whole cloud after switch-off, with a two-dimensional Gaussian fit, rescaling for the cloud extent, and integration. Loading rate and steady-state atom number are extracted from fits to N = R*tau*(1 - exp(-t/tau)), where N is atom number, R is loading rate, tau is characteristic loading time, and t is load time. The spatial scope of each measurement is the complete trapped cloud within the truncated six-beam capture region; horizontal uncertainty reflects the beam truncation measurement, and the paper does not report independent local measurements across the beam aperture.

Observation antibunching with classical light in a linear interferometer

2604.27494
Prediction Quality0.9784

Coverage @2σ1.0000

Categorical Briern/a
Bool/Categorical Accuracyn/a
Formula Accuracy1.0000

Experiment 1

Central concept tested: a modified Hanbury Brown–Twiss interferometer is used to determine whether classical pseudothermal light can exhibit one-zero photon-number-projection antibunching when ordinary single-photon detectors are treated as photon-number-resolving detectors by binning their time-tagged outputs. A single-mode continuous-wave laser at 780 nm center wavelength with 200 kHz frequency bandwidth is incident on a rotating ground glass to generate pseudothermal light; the laser intensity is adjusted with a variable light-intensity attenuator, and the laser spot size on the rotating ground glass is controlled by the lens-to-ground-glass distance. The scattered light is split by a 1:1 non-polarizing beam splitter. Two single-mode-fiber couplers with 4.0 µm core-diameter fibers define the two detection modes and send light to two single-photon detectors D1 and D2. The fiber couplers are placed at symmetric output positions of the beamsplitter for the temporal measurement, with no spatial scan. Photon arrival times from D1 and D2 are recorded on a common clock using a HydraHarp 400 photon detection time-series module, and the offline analysis bins the events into time bins of T = 1 µs. The D1 and D2 single-photon count rates used for this temporal data set are 734.57 kHz and 583.82 kHz, corresponding to 0.73 and 0.58 detected photons per bin and an averaged photon number per detector of \bar n = 0.66. For each integer m = 0, 1, 2, 3, 4, the measured quantity is g_m0^(2)(τ), the normalized correlation between the event “D1 detects m photons in a bin” and “D2 detects zero photons in a bin,” normalized by the corresponding marginal probabilities; τ is the relative delay between detector time bins. The temporal correlation is evaluated over the plotted delay window from −30 µs to +30 µs, and the zero-delay value is obtained from the peak or dip at τ = 0 relative to the long-delay background. The spatial scope is the single pair of symmetric fiber-coupled detection modes at the beamsplitter outputs; the lateral detection-mode aperture is set by the 4.0 µm single-mode fiber cores, and no independent repeated sample count or scan step size is reported.

Experiment 2

Central concept tested: the modified HBT photon-number-projection measurement is compared for pseudothermal light and coherent laser light to determine whether the one-zero projection correlation depends jointly on thermal photon statistics and photon-number binning. The optical layout is the same two-output beamsplitter geometry with two single-mode-fiber-coupled single-photon detectors and HydraHarp 400 time-tag recording. For the pseudothermal-light data, a 780 nm single-mode continuous-wave laser with 200 kHz bandwidth is scattered from a rotating ground glass before a 1:1 non-polarizing beamsplitter; for the laser-light control, the rotating ground glass is removed so that the 780 nm single-mode continuous-wave laser directly enters the interferometer. The variable attenuator sweeps the input intensity, and for every setting the photon records are binned into T = 1 µs time bins. The independent variable \bar n is the average number of detected photons per detector per bin, obtained by averaging the binned counts from D1 and D2. The plotted sweep covers approximately \bar n = 0 to 0.66. The measured quantities are zero-delay normalized photon-number-projection correlations g_m0^(2)(0), where m = 0, 1, 2, 3, 4 for pseudothermal light and m = 1 for the laser-light control; g_m0^(2)(0) is defined as the joint probability for m detections in D1 and zero detections in D2 at zero relative delay divided by the product of the corresponding marginal probabilities. The spatial detection scope is the same fixed pair of fiber-coupled beamsplitter output modes as in the temporal experiment, with 4.0 µm fiber-core modal apertures and no spatial scan; the plotted points correspond to a single intensity sweep, and no independent repeated sample count or scan step size is reported.

Experiment 3

Central concept tested: the modified HBT photon-number-projection scheme is configured to detect spatial one-zero projection correlations of classical pseudothermal light and to determine whether changing the bin-defined average photon number can switch the spatial g_10^(2) feature between suppression and enhancement. A 780 nm single-mode continuous-wave laser with 200 kHz bandwidth illuminates a rotating ground glass to produce pseudothermal light, which is split by a 1:1 non-polarizing beamsplitter. The two output modes are coupled into 4.0 µm core-diameter single-mode fibers and detected by two single-photon detectors whose time tags are recorded by a HydraHarp 400. D2 is held fixed at a reference output position, while D1 is mounted on motorized x-y translation stages and scanned horizontally through the corresponding output beam; the plotted horizontal displacement is Δx from −1.0 mm to +1.0 mm, with Δx = 0 corresponding to the symmetric-position alignment of the two detection modes. The reported scan is a single horizontal line scan through the coupled detection mode; the fiber core sets the lateral modal aperture, while the scan step size, beam waist at the coupler, and number of repeated scans are not specified. For each Δx, g_m0^(2)(Δx) is computed by dividing the local peak or dip value by the far-displacement background for m = 0, 1, 2, 3, 4. Two binned-photon regimes are measured using the same optical geometry: a T = 1 µs bin giving \bar n = 0.66 average detected photons per detector, and a T = 3 µs bin giving \bar n = 1.98 average detected photons per detector; \bar n is calculated by averaging D1 and D2 detected photon numbers.

Direct Time-Domain Observation of l-Doubling via Centrifugal-Distortion Pre-compensation

2605.02512
Prediction Quality0.8641
Numeric rel. CRPS1.0336
Coverage @1σ0.6667
Coverage @2σ0.6667
Bool Brier0.0168
Categorical Brier0.0284
Bool/Categorical Accuracy1.0000
Formula Accuracy0.0000

Experiment 1

Central concept tested: a femtosecond pump pulse with a tailored cubic spectral phase is used to imprint a Raman-coherence phase that cancels centrifugal-distortion dephasing at a chosen rotational revival of a gas-phase molecular rotor. The validation target is methyl iodide (CH3I), a symmetric-top molecule with strong centrifugal distortion and no l-doubling contribution in the alignment signal. CH3I gas is held at 15 torr and room temperature in the spectroscopic gas cell. A Ti:sapphire/CPA ultrafast source provides approximately 800 nm, 110–120 fs pulses. The beam is split into a strong pump arm carrying about 90% of the energy and a weak probe arm carrying about 10%. The pump is sent through a 4-f pulse shaper consisting of two gratings, cylindrical reflective optics, and a Jenoptik dual-mask 640-pixel spatial light modulator located at the Fourier plane. The probe is frequency doubled in a BBO crystal to 400 nm. Pump and probe propagate collinearly through the gas; the birefringence measurement integrates over the pump–probe overlap volume along the gas-cell path, with no spatial scan or lateral/depth-resolved imaging reported. The probe polarization is set to 45 degrees relative to the pump polarization; after the cell the transmitted probe passes through a quarter-wave plate and Wollaston prism and is read by balanced photodetectors to record transient optical birefringence versus pump–probe delay. Reference traces use an unshaped transform-limited near-IR pump. Shaped traces use a spectral phase Phi_shape(omega)=a_tilde(omega-omega0)^2+b_tilde(omega-omega0)^3, where omega is optical angular frequency, omega0 is the pump carrier angular frequency, a_tilde is the quadratic spectral-phase coefficient, and b_tilde is the cubic spectral-phase coefficient. For the analytic cubic-phase design, a_tilde is set to zero and b_tilde is chosen for a target revival order n using the molecular rotational constant B and centrifugal-distortion constant D in consistent spectroscopic units; T_rev=(2Bc)^(-1), with c the speed of light. The reported CH3I constants used for the design context are B=0.25 cm^-1 and D=2.1e-7 cm^-1. Time-domain windows are examined around the 1.5 T_rev, 2.5 T_rev, and 5.5 T_rev fractional revivals. The applied cubic phase coefficients are b_tilde=2.0e7 fs^3 for 1.5 T_rev, b_tilde=3.4e7 fs^3 for 2.5 T_rev, and b_tilde=6.8e7 fs^3 for 5.5 T_rev. The time axis is referenced by setting the first signal peak at the end of the excitation pulse to t=0. The measurement is configured to compare the revival waveform morphology under unshaped excitation with the waveform obtained when the cubic phase is selected to pre-compensate centrifugal distortion at the chosen delay.

Experiment 2

Central concept tested: before applying centrifugal-distortion pre-compensation to resolve closely spaced l-doubling contributions in carbon dioxide, the time-domain width of a conventional CO2 alignment feature is measured to set the temporal scale required for separating different rotational-vibrational components. CO2 gas is measured by time-resolved optical birefringence in the same collinear pump–probe geometry: an approximately 800 nm, 120 fs transform-limited pump excites rotational Raman coherences; a weak 400 nm probe generated by frequency doubling in BBO crosses the same gas volume collinearly; the probe polarization is 45 degrees relative to the pump; a quarter-wave plate, Wollaston prism, and balanced photodetectors analyze the transmitted probe polarization. The measurement integrates over the pump–probe overlap volume in the gas cell; no spatial scan, imaging resolution, or transverse homogeneity map is reported. The pump is unshaped for this reference measurement. The pump–probe delay is scanned around the CO2 three-quarter revival, 3/4 T_rev, using the CO2 rotational timing context given by the B000 ground-state rotational constant. The gas pressure is not specified in the provided text. The measurement is configured to determine the temporal duration of the short-delay alignment transient used as a reference for evaluating resolvability of l-doubling-related alignment events at later revivals.

Experiment 3

Central concept tested: in CO2, l-doubling in the v=1 bending manifold gives multiple rotational-wavepacket contributions with slightly different revival periods, but conventional impulsive alignment can leave those contributions overlapped at long delay because centrifugal distortion broadens the revival waveforms. CO2 gas is placed in the heated spectroscopic cell and measured by collinear time-resolved optical birefringence. An approximately 800 nm, 120 fs transform-limited pump pulse excites rotational alignment without spectral phase shaping. A weak probe arm is frequency doubled in BBO to 400 nm and read out after the sample using 45-degree probe polarization, a quarter-wave plate, a Wollaston prism, and balanced photodetection. The measurement integrates over the pump–probe overlap volume along the cell; no spatially resolved scan or gas-density homogeneity map is reported. The gas is heated to 403 K to increase the thermal population of the v=1 bending manifold associated with l-doubling, while also sampling a higher-J rotational distribution. The CO2 rotational constants used as timing context are B000=0.3902 cm^-1, B010+=0.3912 cm^-1, and B010-=0.3905 cm^-1, with centrifugal-distortion constant D=1.33e-7 cm^-1. The pump–probe delay is scanned near the long-delay 32 T_rev region, around 1380 ps after excitation, where the l-doubling-related revival events are expected to be separated in arrival time by the small differences in rotational constants. The measurement is configured to determine whether the separate rotational-vibrational alignment contributions can be distinguished without centrifugal-distortion pre-compensation.

Experiment 4

Central concept tested: a cubic spectral phase applied to the femtosecond pump pulse is used to pre-compensate centrifugal-distortion dephasing in CO2 at a long-delay revival so that l-doubling-related rotational-vibrational contributions can be directly observed in the time domain. CO2 gas is measured in the heated-cell transient-birefringence apparatus using collinear pump and probe beams. The pump is an approximately 800 nm, 120 fs near-IR pulse routed through the 4-f pulse shaper with gratings, cylindrical reflective optics, and a Jenoptik dual-mask 640-pixel spatial light modulator; its spectral phase is selected according to the cubic pre-compensation design for the 32 T_rev region. The weak probe is generated at 400 nm by frequency doubling in BBO and analyzed after the gas with 45-degree input polarization, a quarter-wave plate, a Wollaston prism, and balanced photodetectors. The measurement integrates over the pump–probe overlap volume in the gas cell; no lateral scan, depth-resolved measurement, or transverse homogeneity assessment is reported. The CO2 pressure is not specified in the provided text. Measurements are taken at both 293 K and 403 K. Heating to 403 K is used as a controlled condition to increase the population of the v=1 bending mode at omega_bend=667 cm^-1. The timing analysis uses the CO2 rotational constants B000=0.3902 cm^-1, B010+=0.3912 cm^-1, and B010-=0.3905 cm^-1, corresponding to the ground vibrational state and the two l-doubled v=1 bending components. The pump–probe delay range recorded for the long-delay measurement is 1380–1412 ps, spanning alignment events around the 32 T_rev to 33 T_rev region. The plotted traces are normalized to the 32 3/4 T_rev revival of the ground-vibrational-level rotational constant B000. The measurement is configured to determine whether the centrifugal-distortion-precompensated response separates the ground-state and l-doubled bending-state contributions in time.

Anderson Localization with Single Photons from a Quantum Emitter

Experiment 1

Fabrication procedure for the integrated photonic platform used to test whether single photons from a solid-state hBN emitter can undergo Anderson localization in a controlled off-diagonally disordered lattice. Two one-dimensional waveguide arrays are inscribed in a fused-silica chip, Corning 7980, by femtosecond direct laser writing. A Coherent Mira/RegA laser provides 800 nm, 150 fs pulses at 100 kHz, focused 200 µm below the sample surface using a 20× microscope objective with numerical aperture NA = 0.36. The substrate is translated relative to the focus with an Aerotech precision positioning system. The ordered control array is designed with 101 nominally identical waveguides separated by 21.9 µm, corresponding to nominal coupling κ0 = 0.08 mm^-1. The disordered array is designed with 101 nominally identical waveguides and inter-waveguide separations chosen so that nearest-neighbor couplings are independently sampled from a uniform distribution κ_n ~ U(κ0-Δ, κ0+Δ), with disorder strength Δ/κ0 = 0.8. The fabricated chip is intended to support end-facet imaging after propagation through the full waveguide length and to provide a periodic control and a coupling-disordered lattice for the Anderson-localization measurements.

Experiment 2

Preparation and optical characterization of the room-temperature hexagonal-boron-nitride single-photon source used to inject nonclassical light into the disordered waveguide lattice. Two drops of solvent-exfoliated hBN solution, Boron Nitride Pristine Flakes in Solution from Graphene Supermarket, are drop-cast onto a marked silicon substrate. The substrate is annealed at 850 °C under a 1 Torr argon atmosphere in a tube furnace and then allowed to cool naturally to room temperature. A single defect is addressed in a custom confocal microscope using a 532 nm continuous-wave laser with 100 µW excitation power, focused by a Nikon 100× objective with NA = 0.9. The hBN sample position is controlled using a P-611.3 NanoCube three-axis piezo scanner with sub-10-nm resolution. Emission is collected through the same objective, separated from excitation using a 70:30 non-polarizing beamsplitter and pump-rejection filtering, and routed either to a spectrometer or to a fiber-based Hanbury Brown–Twiss interferometer. The zero-phonon-line region is isolated using a 20 nm-class bandpass filter centered on the selected emitter line. The second-order autocorrelation g^(2)(t) is normalized at long delay and fit with the three-level model g^(2)(t)=1-(1+a1)exp(-t/τ1)+a2 exp(-t/τ2), where τ1 is the antibunching recovery time associated with the excited-state lifetime under the measurement conditions, τ2 is a bunching timescale, and a1 and a2 are amplitudes. No detector-jitter correction is applied. The measurement is configured to establish the spectral emission line and whether emission is dominated by a single quantum defect for use in the Anderson-localization experiment.

Experiment 3

Ordered-array control measurement for the single-photon transport experiment. Single photons emitted by the characterized hBN defect at its selected zero-phonon line are isolated with a 20 nm bandpass filter, directed through free-space optics, and coupled into the central input waveguide of the 101-site periodic laser-written fused-silica waveguide array with 21.9 µm spacing and nominal coupling κ0 = 0.08 mm^-1. The waveguide chip output facet is imaged using an objective onto a single-photon-resolving electron-multiplying CCD camera. The spatial scope is the full visible end-facet span of the ordered array, approximately 0 to 1200 µm laterally in the plotted line scan; the two-dimensional EMCCD image is vertically binned to obtain a one-dimensional intensity profile across waveguide positions. This control is configured to determine the ordered-lattice transport behavior under the same single-photon source, coupling optics, chip imaging, and end-facet analysis used for the disordered Anderson-localization measurements.

Experiment 4

Bulk Anderson-localization measurement with hBN single photons in the coupling-disordered laser-written waveguide array. Single photons from the room-temperature hBN defect, spectrally filtered around the 619 nm zero-phonon-line region, are coupled by free-space optics into interior sites of the 101-waveguide disordered fused-silica array. The array uses off-diagonal disorder: nearest-neighbor couplings are designed to be uniformly distributed about κ0 with disorder strength Δ/κ0 = 0.8 while on-site propagation constants are nominally fixed. To avoid boundary reflections, the first bulk launch is placed at waveguide 30 of 101 and subsequent realizations are obtained by shifting the excitation site to neighboring waveguides. For each of 30 launch-site/disorder realizations, the output facet after propagation through the chip is imaged on a single-photon-resolving EMCCD. The end-facet intensity is analyzed over waveguide indices centered on the injection site, covering the localized central region and tails across approximately -20 to +20 relative waveguide indices. The peak intensity in each waveguide is extracted from the end-facet image, aligned to the injection site, averaged over the 30 realizations, and fit to I(n)=I0 exp(-2|n-n0|/ξ), where n is relative waveguide index, n0 is the launch index after alignment, I0 is a fitted amplitude, and ξ is the effective localization length in waveguide-site units. The measurement is designed to test whether the limited temporal coherence of a defect-based solid-state emitter is compatible with Anderson localization in an integrated photonic lattice.

Experiment 5

Boundary Anderson-localization measurement with hBN single photons in the same coupling-disordered laser-written waveguide array. The output facet is imaged with the single-photon-resolving EMCCD through the same objective-based imaging system used for the bulk measurement. Four independent edge-excitation realizations are recorded and averaged. The spatial analysis covers the edge site and neighboring sites extending to approximately 30 waveguide indices into the array. Peak intensities are extracted per waveguide, averaged over the four realizations, and fit to I(n)=I0 exp(-2|n-n0|/ξ), where n is waveguide index measured inward from the boundary, n0 is the edge launch site, I0 is a fitted amplitude, and ξ is the effective localization length in waveguide-site units. This measurement probes how Anderson localization of hBN single photons is modified when the injection site has fewer neighboring scattering pathways at the lattice boundary.

Experimental demonstration of a coherent detector blinding attack on a real CV-QKD system

2605.03572
Prediction Quality0.9614
Numeric rel. CRPS0.1253
Coverage @1σ0.3472
Coverage @2σ0.8333
Bool Brier0.0064
Categorical Brier0.0446
Bool/Categorical Accuracy1.0000
Formula Accuracyn/a

Experiment 1

Central concept: a controlled wideband incoherent optical-noise source is used to emulate the excess-noise signature that a collective eavesdropping attack would add to a continuous-variable quantum key distribution (CV-QKD) receiver, so that a later coherent detector-blinding stage can be tested against a known injected-noise baseline. The experiment uses only the Bob-side receiver of a polarization-diverse local-local-oscillator CV-QKD system. The receiver local oscillator is an Aerodiode 1550 nm DFB Model 5 laser driven by a Wavelength Electronics MPL250 laser driver and stabilized by a Wavelength Electronics PTC2.5K-CH temperature controller; its output passes through an optical isolator and an Agiltron LightBend 1x1 PM optical switch into a polarization-diverse receiver. The signal input passes through an Agiltron LightBend 1x1 optical switch and then a PBS that separates polarization components into two polarization-maintaining 50/50 beam splitters. The LO PBS is oriented with its fast axis shifted by 45 degrees relative to the LO polarization so that both receiver arms are illuminated. The two balanced detector channels are Wieserlabs WL-BPD1GA balanced optical receivers with 1.6 GHz bandwidth and DC cut-off below 300 kHz, digitized by a Texas Instruments ADC12DJ3200EVM at 3.2 GHz. The digital processing applied to each acquired horizontal and vertical polarization trace consists of downconversion around 400 MHz to baseband by multiplication with exp(-i 2 pi 400e6 t), root-raised-cosine filtering with roll-off factor 0.95 at 153.6 MBaud, polarization-drift recovery by combining the two polarization components assuming equal distribution between receiver arms, and variance computation. Thermal, shot, and noisy traces are acquired by controlling the receiver optical switches: both paths closed for Var[thermal], LO path open and signal path closed for Var[shot], and both paths open for Var[noisy]. Shot-noise units are defined as SNU = Var[shot] - Var[thermal]. The inferred noise variance is sigma_tilde_squared = Var[noisy]/SNU, the receiver thermal noise is epsilon_th = Var[thermal]/SNU, and the inferred excess noise is epsilon_tilde = (sigma_tilde_squared - 1 - epsilon_th)/(eta*T), using receiver efficiency eta = 0.90 and assumed channel transmission T = 0.5, corresponding to 15 km of standard single-mode fibre. The noise source consists of a Thorlabs EDFA100S erbium-doped fibre amplifier operated without input signal so that its amplified spontaneous emission supplies the incoherent optical noise; this output is attenuated by a Thorlabs V1550A voltage-controlled variable optical attenuator driven by a UNI-T UDP3305S power supply under PC serial control, and then combined through a WBC-2x2-13/15-10/90 90/10 beam splitter. The 90% splitter output is monitored by an OZ-Optics POM-300 optical power meter, with readings converted to the 10% port power using the known splitting ratio. The calibration/reproducibility measurement consists of four independent optical-power sweeps over the noise-source setting, varying the monitored noise power from -13.6 dBm to -51.25 dBm with smaller steps at higher attenuation. The spatial/geometric scope is the full two-arm polarization-diverse receiver input and both balanced-detector outputs after digital polarization recombination; reproducibility is assessed by comparing all four repeated sweeps over the same optical path and receiver processing chain. The measurement is configured to determine whether the ASE source provides a reproducible mapping from optical noise power to inferred CV-QKD excess noise in SNU.

Experiment 2

Central concept: a coherent detector-blinding source is built to force an AC-coupled balanced detector in a real CV-QKD receiver into a nonlinear or clipped response regime. The attack concept uses a detuned optical field to exploit wavelength-dependent imbalance in the receiver optics and uses temporal modulation to pass through the balanced detector AC coupling, rather than relying on a constant optical blinding level. The tested receiver is the same polarization-diverse CV-QKD Bob receiver described in the paper: an Aerodiode 1550 nm DFB local oscillator, PBS splitting into two polarization-maintaining 50/50 beam splitters, two Wieserlabs WL-BPD1GA balanced optical receivers with 1.6 GHz bandwidth and low-frequency cut-off below 300 kHz, and a Texas Instruments ADC12DJ3200EVM digitizer sampling at 3.2 GHz with 12-bit precision. The blinding source consists of a Sumitomo Electric Industries SLV5410 laser diode emitting at 1344 nm. The diode current is supplied by a Rigol DG1032 function generator, enabling direct square-wave optical modulation at 10 MHz, and the diode temperature is controlled with a Thorlabs TED200C temperature controller. The modulated blinding field passes through a Thorlabs V1550A voltage-controlled variable optical attenuator driven by a UNI-T UDP3305S power supply under PC serial control. The attenuated light enters a Thorlabs TD1315R5F1 50/50 dual-window 1310/1550 nm beam splitter; one output is monitored by an OZ Optics POM-600 optical power meter and the other is combined with the signal fibre through a Thorlabs WD1350B wavelength-division multiplexer. A fibre polarization controller after the multiplexer is adjusted while monitoring both balanced-detector outputs so that the blinding-field amplitude delivered to the two polarization receiver arms is matched. For the standalone blinding-response measurement, the horizontal balanced-detector arm is monitored in the time domain while the blinding optical power is swept from -15 dBm to -13 dBm. The measurement scope is the horizontal polarization receiver arm and its ADC output over repeated cycles of the 10 MHz blinding modulation; the two-arm balance of the injected blinding field is checked by comparing the output amplitudes of both balanced receivers during polarization adjustment. The measurement is configured to identify the receiver stage that limits the response and to determine the blinding level at which the horizontal-channel digitized output reaches full-scale clipping.

Experiment 3

Central concept: the complete coherent detector-blinding attack is tested on a real CV-QKD receiver by combining an ASE noise source, representing the excess-noise impact of a collective attack, with a modulated detuned blinding source that drives the receiver out of its normal linear operating regime. The apparatus is a reduced Bob-only implementation of the attack scenario: the exploitation stage is represented by the calibrated EDFA-based incoherent-noise source and the enabling stage is represented by the 1344 nm modulated blinding source. The receiver is the polarization-diverse local-local-oscillator CV-QKD receiver with Aerodiode 1550 nm DFB LO, Agiltron optical switches for thermal/shot/noisy acquisitions, PBS-based polarization splitting, two polarization-maintaining 50/50 beam splitters, two Wieserlabs WL-BPD1GA balanced optical receivers with 1.6 GHz bandwidth and 300 kHz low-frequency cut-off, and a Texas Instruments ADC12DJ3200EVM digitizer at 3.2 GHz. Digital processing consists of 400 MHz frequency recovery, root-raised-cosine filtering with roll-off 0.95 and 153.6 MBaud symbol rate, two-polarization combination for polarization-drift recovery, and variance computation. Shot-noise units are defined as SNU = Var[shot] - Var[thermal], with epsilon_tilde = (Var[noisy]/SNU - 1 - Var[thermal]/SNU)/(eta*T), eta = 0.90, and assumed channel transmission T = 0.5. The noise source is the Thorlabs EDFA100S ASE source attenuated by a Thorlabs V1550A VOA and injected through the 90/10 splitter path; the fixed corrected noise powers used in the attack measurement are -27.0 dBm, -28.3 dBm, -29.9 dBm, -30.8 dBm, and -31.8 dBm. The blinding source is the Sumitomo Electric Industries SLV5410 1344 nm diode directly square-wave modulated at 10 MHz by a Rigol DG1032, temperature controlled by a Thorlabs TED200C, attenuated by a Thorlabs V1550A VOA, monitored after a Thorlabs TD1315R5F1 50/50 beam splitter with an OZ Optics POM-600 optical power meter, and injected through a Thorlabs WD1350B wavelength-division multiplexer followed by a polarization controller adjusted to balance the blinding amplitudes in the receiver arms. The independent blinding-power sweep spans -15.64536 dBm to -12.53858 dBm for each fixed noise-source setting. The measurement scope is the complete two-balanced-detector polarization-diverse receiver after digital polarization recombination, with five independent fixed injected-noise conditions compared across the same blinding-power sweep. For evaluating whether a blinded receiver would accept a key, the receiver-estimated excess noise is compared with the positive-key-rate excess-noise threshold computed using the Denys-Brown-Leverrier arbitrary-modulation security proof for a probabilistically shaped 64-QAM constellation with Maxwell-Boltzmann-distributed constellation points at T = 0.5. The reported hiding metric is the minimum blinding optical power at which the receiver-estimated excess noise for a given calibrated injected excess noise falls below that positive-key-rate threshold.

Pair-Breaking and Dimensionality in Spin-Orbit Coupled Superconductors

2605.06514
Prediction Quality0.9416
Numeric rel. CRPS0.1904

Coverage @2σ0.6667

Categorical Briern/a

Experiment 1

Central concept: the zero-field superconducting transition of thickness-controlled LaBi2 thin films supplies the baseline critical temperature used to assess Pauli-limit violation and to compare KLB and Kharitonov-Feigel'man (KF) pair-breaking models. Measure rectangular Ge-capped LaBi2/MgO(001) films with approximate XRD-derived thicknesses t = 75, 34, 12, 8.5, 3.0, and 2.1 nm in a dilution refrigerator with base temperature about 20 mK. Use a linear four-point bond geometry on each rectangular film; drive an AC bias current of 100-500 nA at 10-20 Hz and read the longitudinal resistance with a lock-in amplifier. Sweep temperature through the superconducting transition at zero applied magnetic field. Normalize resistance by the normal-state resistance R0 measured above the transition, and define the experimental zero-field critical temperature T_c,H=0^Exp as the temperature where R/R0 = 0.5. The contacted transport path spans the macroscopic rectangular sample between voltage leads; the paper does not report the channel dimensions, contact spacing, lateral homogeneity map, or number of independent locations per thickness. The goal is to quantify how the intrinsic superconducting transition evolves as the film approaches the two-dimensional dirty-limit regime relevant to spin-orbit and magnetic pair-breaking.

Experiment 2

Central concept: perpendicular-field superconducting transport supplies the superconducting coherence length used to assess whether LaBi2 films lie in the dirty limit, where the KLB and KF pair-breaking frameworks are applicable. Use the same Ge-capped LaBi2/MgO(001) rectangular films and four-point lock-in transport configuration as the zero-field transition experiment, in a dilution refrigerator with base temperature about 20 mK. Apply magnetic field perpendicular to the film plane and sweep temperature through the resistive transition for each thickness t = 75, 34, 12, 8.5, 3.0, and 2.1 nm. Determine H_c2^perp(T) from the same resistive transition criterion used for the main transport analysis. Fit H_c2^perp(T) to the single-gap Ginzburg-Landau relation H_c2^perp(T) = (Phi0/(2*pi*xi_ab^2)) * (1 - (T_c/T_c,H=0)^2), where Phi0 is the superconducting flux quantum, xi_ab is the in-plane superconducting coherence length, T_c is the transition temperature at applied perpendicular field, and T_c,H=0 is the zero-field transition temperature. The measurement samples the contacted rectangular transport path; the paper does not report channel dimensions, lateral mapping, or multiple-location statistics. The goal is to obtain the superconducting coherence length for comparison with mean free path estimates and to determine the dimensional/scattering regime relevant to pair-breaking.

Experiment 3

Central concept: normal-state magnetotransport provides carrier-density, mobility, mean-free-path, Fermi-momentum, and transport-scattering estimates needed to evaluate the orbital pair-breaking term in the KF model for finite-thickness LaBi2. Measure normal-state sheet resistivity rho_xx and Hall resistivity rho_yx at T = 2 K for Ge-capped LaBi2/MgO(001) films, including representative thicknesses 75, 12, and 2.3 nm, under perpendicular magnetic field swept from 0 to 10 T. Use the same macroscopic rectangular four-point geometry; the paper does not report channel dimensions or lateral mapping. Fit rho_xx(B) and rho_yx(B) to the two-carrier Drude formulas rho_xx = (1/e)*[(p*mu_h+n*mu_e)+(p*mu_e+n*mu_h)*mu_h*mu_e*B^2]/[(p*mu_h+n*mu_e)^2+(p-n)^2*mu_h^2*mu_e^2*B^2] and rho_yx = (B/e)*[(p*mu_h^2-n*mu_e^2)+(p-n)^2*mu_h^2*mu_e^2*B^2]/[(p*mu_h+n*mu_e)^2+(p-n)^2*mu_h^2*mu_e^2*B^2], where e is elementary charge, B is perpendicular magnetic field, n and p are electron-like and hole-like carrier densities, and mu_e and mu_h are electron-like and hole-like mobilities. Estimate mean free path and transport scattering time using l_i = (mu_i/e)*p_F,i = (hbar*mu_i/e)*(3*pi^2*n_i)^(1/3) and tau_tr,i = m_eff,i*mu_i/e, with m_eff,i set equal to the free-electron mass m_e for both carrier types. For the result expressed as a formula, t denotes film thickness in nm and <n> denotes the species-averaged carrier density mean(n,p) in cm^-3. The goal is to characterize the normal-state transport inputs used for the finite-dimensional orbital depairing analysis.

Experiment 4

Central concept: the in-plane upper critical field H_c2^parallel(T) is the primary measurement used to test whether finite-thickness orbital depairing and magnetic spin-exchange scattering, included in the KF framework, are required beyond the KLB spin-orbit-scattering-only model. Measure Ge-capped LaBi2/MgO(001) films with thicknesses t = 75, 34, 12, 8.5, 3.0, and 2.1 nm in a dilution refrigerator with base temperature about 20 mK and a two-axis vector magnet rated 9-3 T. Use a field-alignment procedure adopted from the cited LaSb2 work to align the magnetic field parallel to the film plane. Use linear four-point lock-in transport with 100-500 nA excitation at 10-20 Hz on rectangular samples. Sweep temperature through the superconducting transition while applying parallel magnetic fields over the accessible range; determine H_c2^parallel(T) using the R/R0 = 0.5 criterion, with additional 0.1 R0 and 0.9 R0 criteria checked for the 2.1 nm film. The active measurement region is the contacted macroscopic transport path; channel dimensions, edge distances, and lateral homogeneity maps are not reported. Define the conventional weak-coupling BCS Pauli field as H_P^BCS = 1.86*T_c, with T_c in kelvin and H_P^BCS in tesla. Define the Pauli-limit violation ratio as H_c2^parallel(T -> 0)/H_P^BCS. For the power-law result, t is film thickness in nm and alpha is defined by H_c2^parallel/H_P^BCS proportional to t^alpha. The measurement is configured to detect thickness-dependent suppression of orbital pair breaking and possible field-enhanced superconductivity from suppression of magnetic fluctuations in the ultrathin dirty-limit regime.

Scalable Liquid-Crystal Integrated Silicon Nitride Photonic Circuits for Reconfigurable Quantum Interference

Experiment 1

The central device concept is a silicon-nitride Mach-Zehnder interferometer (MZI) whose relative optical phase is tuned by reorienting a nematic liquid-crystal (LC) layer above a waveguide, targeting compact low-voltage reconfigurable quantum interference. For the electron-beam-defined devices used in the quantum measurements, fabricate the structure on a SiO2-on-Si wafer carrying a 300 nm LPCVD SiN layer. Define the LC-MZI waveguide pattern by electron-beam lithography using a JEOL JBX-8100FS, form 1 µm-wide fully etched SiN waveguides with 300 nm etch depth by reactive-ion etching, and deposit a 2 µm PECVD SiO2 upper cladding. Open a 3 µm-wide, 500 µm-long trench in the SiO2 cladding above the phase-shifter waveguide using 405 nm maskless lithography followed by reactive-ion etching. Deposit Cr/Au electrodes by e-beam evaporation and lift-off, pattern SU-8 microfluidic walls around the LC trench, dice a quartz lid to match the SU-8 structure, spin-coat and rub a polyimide alignment layer on the quartz to define the LC molecular orientation, align and bond the quartz lid to the SU-8 with UV-curable epoxy, and inject Merck E7 nematic liquid crystal into the sealed cavity by capillary action. Assess whether the target LC-integrated MZI structure is formed using an optical microscope image over the assembled circuit with a 2 mm scale reference, a cross-sectional SEM image of the LC trench/waveguide region before LC injection with a 1 µm scale reference, and crossed-polarized optical microscopy over the waveguide/electrode region with a 200 µm scale reference for rubbing directions parallel to and 45° relative to the polarizer. The goal is to determine whether the fabrication-and-assembly procedure produces the intended LC-filled, aligned SiN MZI phase-modulator geometry needed for voltage-controlled quantum interference, without using the downstream optical or quantum measurements as the criterion.

Experiment 2

To test the operating mechanism of the LC phase shifter used for the reconfigurable SiN quantum-interference circuit, examine the LC-filled phase-modulator region by crossed-polarized optical microscopy while applying an AC square-wave voltage to the electrodes. Use the Merck E7 nematic liquid crystal in the trench above the SiN waveguide, with ordinary and extraordinary refractive indices specified for the design as no = 1.500 and ne = 1.685 at 1550 nm. Avoid DC driving; use an AC square wave in the approximately 500 Hz to 1 kHz range to reduce ionic-current screening. Configure the crossed polarizer/analyzer microscope so that the optical transmission through the LC region is sensitive to birefringence-induced polarization rotation. Compare the same phase-modulator region, imaged with a 30 µm scale reference near the waveguide and electrodes, at VLC = 0 V and at VLC = 3 V. The goal is to determine whether the electric field from the electrodes changes the LC molecular orientation in the waveguide-overlap region, which is the physical mechanism intended to tune the guided-mode effective index and hence the MZI phase.

Experiment 3

Characterize the static electro-optic phase-shifting performance of the electron-beam-fabricated LC-MZI, which implements voltage-controlled phase tuning for quantum interference by changing the LC-clad SiN waveguide effective index. Use MZIs composed of two 2×2 multimode-interference couplers and an LC phase-modulator section inside SU-8 walls. The SiN waveguide cross section is 300 nm high and 1 µm wide, the SiO2 upper cladding is 2 µm thick outside the LC trench, and the LC interaction trench over the waveguide is 3 µm wide and 500 µm long. Apply an AC square-wave voltage to the LC electrodes in the approximately 500 Hz to 1 kHz range. For the unbalanced MZI, measure the S42 optical transmission spectrum by sweeping wavelength from 1500 nm to 1600 nm at VLC = 0 V and at a voltage selected to shift the interferometric fringe by a half period. For the balanced MZI with the same 500 µm modulator length, measure the S32 optical transmission spectrum over 1540 nm to 1580 nm at five applied voltages spanning 0 V to the switching-voltage range. Define Vpi as the voltage required to produce a π optical phase shift in the LC phase shifter, and define Vpi·L as Vpi multiplied by the LC modulator length L expressed in millimetres. Determine the extinction ratio of the balanced MZI from the maximum-to-minimum transmission contrast in the voltage-tuned spectral response. The measurement targets CMOS-compatible, low-voltage phase modulation in the LC-integrated SiN MZI, not thermal phase tuning.

Experiment 4

Measure the temporal switching response of the electron-beam-fabricated LC-MZI phase modulator to determine whether the LC-based SiN platform is suitable for circuit reconfiguration and active phase stabilization in quantum photonic circuits. Experiment 5

Before using the integrated LC-MZI for on-chip quantum interference, benchmark the indistinguishability of the photon-pair source with a fiber beam splitter. Generate photon pairs by spontaneous parametric down-conversion in a 7 mm-long type-II periodically poled potassium titanyl phosphate crystal pumped by a 780 nm continuous-wave tunable diode laser (Toptica TA Pro). Separate the pair with a polarizing beam splitter, reject residual pump light using long-pass filters, and independently prepare the polarization in each arm using a half-wave plate and a quarter-wave plate. Use a translation stage in one path to sweep the relative arrival delay through the Hong-Ou-Mandel interference region. Couple the photons through fiber optics to a fiber beam splitter and detect the outputs with superconducting nanowire single-photon detectors. Record photon arrival times with a quTAG time-to-digital converter for time-correlated single-photon counting. Compute the accidental-corrected Hong-Ou-Mandel visibility as V = (Cmax - Cmin)/(Cmax + Cmin), where Cmax is the coincidence rate away from temporal overlap and Cmin is the coincidence rate at the interference minimum after accidental-coincidence correction. This control experiment targets the source quality required for later voltage-controlled two-photon interference in the LC-integrated SiN circuit.

Experiment 6

Test voltage-controlled on-chip two-photon interference in the electron-beam-fabricated LC-MZI, the central demonstration that LC phase tuning in a SiN circuit preserves quantum coherence while reconfiguring the two-photon output state. Generate photon pairs in a 7 mm-long type-II PPKTP crystal pumped by a 780 nm continuous-wave tunable diode laser, separate them with a polarizing beam splitter, reject pump light with long-pass filters, set polarization with half-wave and quarter-wave plates, and use a translation stage to control the relative delay. Couple the two photons into the two input ports of the LC-MZI with lensed fibers and collect both output ports with lensed fibers. Use polarization controllers to optimize collection, superconducting nanowire single-photon detectors for detection, and a quTAG time-to-digital converter for time-correlated coincidence counting. The on-chip device contains two 2×2 multimode-interference couplers and a 500 µm-long LC phase shifter in one MZI arm; the LC is driven by an AC square-wave voltage in the approximately 500 Hz to 1 kHz range. First set the relative delay to zero path difference, τ = 0, so that the photons arrive simultaneously at the interferometer, and sweep the LC voltage over the plotted modulation range to compare the voltage dependence of two-photon coincidences with the single-photon cross-port transmission. Then perform full delay-line sweeps at fixed LC voltages including 1.06 V, 1.22 V, and 1.40 V. Define the two-photon interference visibility using the same normalized coincidence-contrast convention V = (Cmax - Cmin)/(Cmax + Cmin), with Cmax and Cmin taken from the fitted delay-dependent coincidence curve at each fixed voltage. The measurement scope is the integrated MZI active region between input ports 1 and 2 and output ports 3 and 4, with the two-photon interaction occurring at the on-chip interferometer while phase is tuned by the LC layer.

Experiment 7

Realization of Backward Retrieval in a Stark-modulated Spin-wave Quantum Memory

2605.11786
Prediction Quality0.8678
Numeric rel. CRPS0.3965
Coverage @1σ0.2808
Coverage @2σ0.8341
Bool Briern/a
Categorical Briern/a
Bool/Categorical Accuracyn/a

Experiment 1

Central concept: implement a Stark-echo-modulated spin-wave quantum memory in Eu3+:Y2SiO5, using electric-field-induced opposite Stark phases in inversion-related Eu3+ sub-ensembles to suppress unwanted coherent echo emission without removing absorbers, thereby preserving the full optical depth for forward or backward retrieval. The preparation procedure is performed on a 0.1% Eu3+:Y2SiO5 crystal with dimensions 5 mm × 5 mm × 10 mm along the D1, D2, and C2 axes, using Eu3+ ions at site 1. The crystal is cooled to 1.8 K in a cryostat. Optical fields propagate along the C2 axis and are polarized along D1. Copper electrodes attached to the crystal with silver paint provide electric fields parallel to D1. A collinear counter-propagating optical arrangement is aligned with a fiber coupler; optical pulses are generated and modulated with two acousto-optic modulators, and avalanche-photodiode detection is gated with a separate acousto-optic modulator. The ensemble-preparation recipe consists of repeated class cleaning and spin-polarization pulse sequences: 1-ms frequency-chirped optical pulses with 100 µW peak power are applied, with the class-cleaning block repeated 200 times and the spin-polarization blocks repeated 200 times as indicated for the addressed Eu3+ hyperfine transitions. The spatial scope is the optical mode passing through the full 10-mm crystal length along C2, targeting the ensemble absorption feature used as the active memory region rather than isolated microscopic sites. The goal is to determine whether this preparation creates a spectrally isolated absorption feature suitable for the Stark-echo memory protocol, quantified by optical depth and spectral bandwidth from the transmission spectrum.

prepared_absorption_feature_optical_depthThe optical depth of the prepared Eu3+ absorption feature used as the active memory transition.1.30.8p10/p50/p90 = 0.45 / 0.8 / 1.4 (normal, σ ≈ 0.371 linear)z = +1.35 (σ = 0.371 normal)
prepared_absorption_feature_bandwidth_MHzThe spectral bandwidth of the prepared Eu3+ absorption feature used for memory operation.2.01.2p10/p50/p90 = 0.6 / 1.2 / 2.4 (log_normal, σ ≈ 0.235 dex)z = +0.94 (σ = 0.235 log_normal)

Experiment 2

Central concept: characterize the Stark control mechanism required for a Stark-echo-modulated spin-wave quantum memory in Eu3+:Y2SiO5, where inversion-related Eu3+ sub-ensembles acquire opposite Stark phases so that a first electric-field pulse can scramble collective emission and a second electric-field pulse can rephase it. The measurement uses the same 0.1% Eu3+:Y2SiO5 crystal, 5 mm × 5 mm × 10 mm along D1, D2, and C2, cooled to 1.8 K, with optical fields propagating along C2 and polarized along D1. Electric fields are applied parallel to D1 using copper electrodes attached with silver paint. The spectrally prepared ensemble from class cleaning and spin polarization is probed over the optical beam path through the full crystal length. Stark modulation is measured with two-pulse photon-echo, four-level photon-echo, and Stark-echo sequences. In the Stark-echo verification, one Stark pulse at time t1 is fixed and the second Stark-pulse area at time t7 is swept; the memory operating point uses a Stark-pulse area of 9.25 V cm^-1 µs. The Stark shift convention is Δν = −L Δµ·E, where Δν is the optical-frequency shift, L is the local-field correction factor, Δµ is the difference in permanent electric dipole moments between the two levels, and E is the applied electric field. For a Stark pulse of duration τS and electric field ES, the two inversion-related sub-ensembles accumulate relative phase 2φS = 2(Δµ·ES/ℏ)τS. The goal is to calibrate the linear Stark coefficient that determines the phase scrambling and rephasing used in the memory sequence.

Experiment 3

Central concept: test forward retrieval in the Stark-echo spin-wave quantum memory, in which four optical π pulses and two Stark pulses are used to suppress the ordinary four-level echo and retrieve a Stark echo while preserving the full prepared optical depth of the Eu3+:Y2SiO5 ensemble. The active medium is a 0.1% Eu3+:Y2SiO5 crystal, 5 mm × 5 mm × 10 mm along D1, D2, and C2, cooled to 1.8 K. Eu3+ site-1 hyperfine levels form the four-level scheme. Optical pulses propagate along C2 with polarization along D1. The electric field is applied along D1 through copper electrodes attached with silver paint. The prepared absorption feature has its optical mode extending through the 10-mm crystal length along C2. The forward-retrieval sequence uses an input signal at t0 followed by a first Stark pulse at t1, optical π pulses at t2 and t3 to transfer optical coherence to the ground-state spin coherence |±1/2>g ↔ |±3/2>g, optical π pulses at t5 and t6 to transfer through the excited-state spin coherence |±3/2>e ↔ |±5/2>e, and a second Stark pulse at t7. The Stark pulse area used for memory operation is 9.25 V cm^-1 µs. The forward phase-matching condition is k_echo = k0 − k2 − k3 + k5 + k6, where ki is the wave vector of the optical pulse at time ti; the echo time is t8 = t0 − t2 − t3 + t5 + t6. Direct avalanche-photodiode detection is used for the retrieved Stark-echo intensity, with the detection window gated by an acousto-optic modulator. The timing of the π pulses is adjusted to temporally separate the Stark echo from four-level-echo background; the reported memory-efficiency point is evaluated at a storage/retrieval time of 29 µs. Coherence dynamics are measured by heterodyne detection of Stark-echo decay curves while independently varying the intervals t6−t3 and t5−t2 over the plotted range from about 20 µs to 60 µs. The data are fitted with ηSE = ηretrieval ηpm ηcontrol^4 ηdecay, where ηSE is total memory efficiency, ηretrieval is the optical-depth-dependent retrieval factor, ηpm is phase-matching efficiency, ηcontrol is the population-transfer efficiency of each π pulse, and ηdecay is the decoherence term. For forward retrieval ηretrieval = d^2 exp(−d), with d = αL the optical depth. The decoherence model is ηdecay = exp[−Γ13^2(t5−t2)^2/(2 ln2/π^2)] exp[−Γ35^2(t6−t3)^2/(2 ln2/π^2) − 2γ(t6−t3)], where Γ13 is the inhomogeneous linewidth of |±1/2>g ↔ |±3/2>g, Γ35 is the inhomogeneous linewidth of |±3/2>e ↔ |±5/2>e, and γ is the effective optical decoherence rate. For the co-propagating forward geometry, ηpm is taken as unity when extracting ηcontrol.

Experiment 4

Central concept: test backward retrieval in the Stark-echo spin-wave quantum memory, using Stark phase scrambling and rephasing to enable a retrieval direction that avoids the reabsorption-limited forward-emission geometry while retaining the full prepared optical depth of the Eu3+:Y2SiO5 ensemble. The active medium is a 0.1% Eu3+:Y2SiO5 crystal, 5 mm × 5 mm × 10 mm along D1, D2, and C2, cooled to 1.8 K. Optical fields address Eu3+ site-1 hyperfine transitions, propagate along the C2 axis in a collinear counter-propagating geometry, and are polarized along D1. Electric fields are applied along D1 using copper electrodes attached with silver paint. The optical mode passes through the 10-mm crystal length and defines the active memory region. The backward-retrieval pulse sequence contains the same Stark-echo elements as the forward protocol: input signal at t0, first Stark pulse at t1, two optical π pulses at t2 and t3, two later optical π pulses at t5 and t6, and a second Stark pulse at t7, with the memory Stark-pulse area set to 9.25 V cm^-1 µs. To target backward phase matching, the propagation direction of the π pulse at t5 is reversed, the two π pulses at t5 and t6 are interchanged relative to the forward sequence, and the timing is adjusted to separate background four-level echoes from the Stark echo by spatial and temporal discrimination. The phase-matching convention is k_echo = k0 − k2 − k3 + k5 + k6, where ki is the optical wave vector at ti; the echo time is t8 = t0 − t2 − t3 + t5 + t6. Direct avalanche-photodiode detection measures the retrieved Stark-echo intensity, and heterodyne detection records Stark-echo decay profiles while varying t6−t3 and t5−t2 over the plotted range from about 25 µs to 60 µs. The reported memory-efficiency point is evaluated at 23 µs. Decay data are fitted with ηSE = ηretrieval ηpm ηcontrol^4 ηdecay, with backward retrieval factor ηretrieval = [1 − exp(−d)]^2, d = αL, and ηdecay = exp[−Γ13^2(t5−t2)^2/(2 ln2/π^2)] exp[−Γ35^2(t6−t3)^2/(2 ln2/π^2) − 2γ(t6−t3)], where Γ13 and Γ35 are the ground- and excited-state spin-transition inhomogeneous linewidths and γ is the effective optical decoherence rate.

Experiment 5

Central concept: quantify coherent-noise pathways in the Stark-echo memory and test whether Stark phase control suppresses unwanted photon-echo and free-induction components that would otherwise contaminate quantum storage. The experiment uses the same prepared 0.1% Eu3+:Y2SiO5 crystal ensemble at 1.8 K, with optical propagation along C2, polarization along D1, and electric-field pulses along D1. The memory sequence is run without an input signal so that any detected free-induction decay or two-pulse-echo noise originates from the applied optical π pulses. The control pulses address the relevant ωb and ωd transitions in the four-level Eu3+ hyperfine scheme. Detection is performed with a gated avalanche photodiode over the output time window following the final π pulse and around the expected two-pulse echo. Measurements are compared for the same optical pulse sequence with and without the Stark electric-field pulse at t7. The spatial scope is the full optical mode through the prepared ensemble; no position-resolved imaging is involved. The intended metric is the residual coherent noise fraction relative to the corresponding no-Stark-field pulse sequence.

Experiment 6

Central concept: use measured four-level-echo noise in the Stark-echo memory to infer control-pulse transfer efficiency and the backward phase-matching limitation. The same 0.1% Eu3+:Y2SiO5 crystal ensemble is prepared by class cleaning and spin polarization, cooled to 1.8 K, and driven with optical pulses along the C2 axis with D1 polarization; electric fields are applied along D1. In the forward and backward Stark-echo memory geometries, an input signal at t0 and four π pulses at t2, t3, t5, and t6 can generate parasitic four-level echoes 4LE0mn when the input excitation combines with π pulses at tm and tn. The four-level-echo wave-vector condition is k4LE = −k0 + kπ1 + kπ2, where k0 is the input-signal wave vector and kπ1 and kπ2 are the wave vectors of the two π pulses forming the parasitic echo. In the forward sequence, the detectable parasitic echoes include 4LE026, 4LE035, and 4LE056; in the backward sequence, the geometry produces two backward-propagating four-level echoes and one forward-propagating four-level echo. Avalanche-photodiode detection records the Stark echo and the temporally separated four-level-echo backgrounds, with the timing set so that the Stark echo and parasitic echoes occupy distinct detection windows. The inference uses the intensity scalings ISE ∝ ηcontrol^4 and I4LE ∝ (1−ηcontrol)^2 ηcontrol^2, where ISE is Stark-echo intensity, I4LE is four-level-echo noise intensity, and ηcontrol is the transfer efficiency of each π pulse; the relevant decoherence factors are included. For the backward sequence, the inferred ηcontrol is combined with the measured backward efficiency and decay model to estimate ηpm, the spatial phase-matching efficiency of the counter-propagating geometry.

Experiment 7

Central concept: evaluate whether the Stark-echo spin-wave memory preserves time-bin photonic qubits with high conditional fidelity for both forward and backward retrieval while suppressing coherent noise through Stark modulation. The memory uses the same 0.1% Eu3+:Y2SiO5 crystal at 1.8 K, Eu3+ site-1 hyperfine transitions, optical propagation along C2 with D1 polarization, and electric-field pulses along D1. The input qubit is |ψ> = (|e> + exp(iΔα)|l>)/sqrt(2), where |e> and |l> are the early and late temporal modes and Δα is their relative phase. Forward-retrieval qubit measurements use a time-bin separation Δt = 4 µs; backward-retrieval qubit measurements use Δt = 5 µs. Basis-state fidelities Fe and Fl are calculated from signal-to-noise ratio as Fe,l = (S+N)/(S+2N), where S is the signal after subtracting noise and N is the noise. The average computational-basis fidelity is Fpoles = (Fe+Fl)/2. For superposition-state measurements, an unbalanced Mach-Zehnder-interferometer-type pulse sequence is implemented by replacing the π pulse at t6 with two π/2 pulses separated by the same Δt and with relative phase Δβ. The phase Δβ is swept to measure interference for input states with Δα = 0° and Δα = 90°. The visibility V of the interference fringe gives the equatorial-state fidelity Fequator = (1+V)/2. The total conditional fidelity per qubit is FT = (1/3)Fpoles + (2/3)Fequator. The measurement integrates over the optical memory mode through the full prepared ensemble and compares forward and backward retrieval configurations under their respective phase-matching geometries.

Observation of end-to-end pumping in a quasiperiodic Fibonacci-type photonic chain

2605.13116
Prediction Quality0.9288
Numeric rel. CRPS0.0885
Coverage @1σ0.9286
Coverage @2σ1.0000
