Categorical Briern/a
Bool/Categorical Accuracy1.0000
Formula Accuracy0.0000

Experiment 1

Fabricate the photonic platform for a minimal-control quasiperiodic Fibonacci-type end-to-end pump: an array of N = 11 evanescently coupled optical waveguides in transparent glass, where nearest-neighbor separations implement a Fibonacci-type Sturmian sequence with two target center-to-center spacings dA = 10 µm and dB = 7 µm. The sequence is defined by χ_i = sgn[cos(2π i τ^{-1} + π τ^{-1} + φ) − cos(π τ^{-1})], where τ = (1 + sqrt(5))/2 is the golden ratio, φ is the phason angle, positive χ_i assigns spacing dB, negative χ_i assigns spacing dA, and i labels neighboring waveguide bonds. The central device idea is that two selected phason configurations support a localized defect-like pumping state at opposite ends, so the platform should realize uniform single-mode guides whose separations can encode the quasiperiodic hopping pattern and allow only waveguides 2 and 10 to be bent for pumping. Use femtosecond-laser direct writing with a Menlo Systems BlueCut fs laser at 1030 nm central wavelength, approximately 350 fs pulse duration, and 1 MHz repetition rate. Focus the writing beam into the bulk glass with a 40× objective of NA = 0.6. Mount the substrate on a high-precision three-axis translation stage and translate it along the propagation direction z at 40 mm/s while keeping the laser focus fixed. Write all guides at approximately 100 µm depth below the glass surface. Set the writing laser power to the paper's optimized value for single-mode guiding at the 780 nm probe wavelength; the numerical writing power is not reported. Fabricate two nominally identical samples for each reported lattice configuration, including defect-free, bent, straight, and structurally defected arrays, so the spatial scope is the full 11-waveguide chain in each sample and reproducibility is assessed by independent fabrication and optical measurement of paired samples.

Experiment 2

Calibrate the evanescent coupling law used to map the quasiperiodic Fibonacci-type photonic chain onto a tight-binding Hamiltonian for the minimal end-to-end pump. Fabricate isolated two-waveguide directional couplers in the same transparent glass substrate and with the same femtosecond direct-writing conditions as the full N = 11 lattices: 1030 nm, approximately 350 fs, 1 MHz writing laser; 40× NA = 0.6 focusing objective; 40 mm/s sample translation; approximately 100 µm writing depth; and the same optimized writing power used for the pump arrays. Prepare couplers with different center-to-center separations d_ij spanning the range shown in the calibration plot, approximately 9–16 µm. Inject 780 nm light into one waveguide of each two-guide coupler and measure the two output intensities at the end facet. Extract the coupling strength J_ij in mm^{-1} from the power oscillation according to coupled-mode theory. Fit the measured coupling-versus-distance data to an exponential relation J_ij(d_ij) = J exp(−d_ij/ξ), where d_ij is the center-to-center separation in µm, J is the fitted prefactor in mm^{-1}, and ξ is the fitted evanescent decay length in µm. This calibration supplies the physical hopping parameters for the Fibonacci-type chain in which dA = 10 µm represents a weak bond and dB = 7 µm represents a strong bond.

Experiment 3

Optically test the localized end states that underpin the proposed minimal-control quasiperiodic Fibonacci-type pump, using straight 11-waveguide arrays with no z-dependent bending. Fabricate two static Fibonacci-type configurations: configuration 1 is the phason configuration in which the localized pumping state is targeted at the left end, and configuration 2 is the phason configuration in which the corresponding localized pumping state is targeted at the right end. The arrays use dA = 10 µm and dB = 7 µm center-to-center spacings according to the Sturmian Fibonacci construction, with all waveguide separations kept constant along the full propagation length L = 40 mm. Use the same femtosecond-laser-written glass waveguides described for the platform fabrication. For optical characterization, couple 780 nm laser light into a single outermost guide using a 10× objective with NA = 0.25: inject the leftmost waveguide i = 1 for configuration 1 and the rightmost waveguide i = 11 for configuration 2. Image the entire output facet covering all 11 waveguides onto a CCD camera with a second 10× NA = 0.25 objective matched to the input objective. Record the output intensity distribution across all waveguide sites i = 1,...,11. The measurement is configured to determine whether single-site injection into the outer guide efficiently overlaps the localized defect-like pumping state without performing end-to-end transfer; two nominally identical samples per configuration are used to check reproducibility.

Experiment 4

Demonstrate the central device concept: end-to-end light pumping in a finite quasiperiodic Fibonacci-type photonic chain by changing only a small part of the structure. Use an N = 11 femtosecond-laser-written glass waveguide array of total propagation length L = 40 mm, with Fibonacci-type separations dA = 10 µm and dB = 7 µm. The pump interpolates between configuration 1, with the localized defect-like pumping state targeted at the left edge, and configuration 2, with that state targeted at the right edge. Only waveguides 2 and 10 are laterally bent along z, which changes the two outer bonds at each end. In the theoretical protocol the distances are d_1,9(z) = dB − (dB − dA) cos(π z / 2L) and d_2,10(z) = dA + (dB − dA) cos(π z / 2L), where d_1 ≡ d_12, d_2 ≡ d_23, d_9 ≡ d_9,10, d_10 ≡ d_10,11, z is the propagation coordinate, and L is the total propagation length. In the fabricated device implement this interpolation as an S-bend trajectory made of two circular arcs with opposite curvature joined at z = L/2, having the same endpoints, vanishing slopes at z = 0 and z = L, and midpoint position as the cosine profile. Use lateral displacement Δx = 3 µm and arc radius R = L^2/(4Δx) ≈ 133 m. Inject 780 nm laser light into the leftmost waveguide i = 1 at z = 0 using a 10× NA = 0.25 objective. Image the complete output facet spanning all 11 waveguides at z = L onto a CCD camera through a matched 10× NA = 0.25 objective. The measurement tests whether single-end excitation is transported to the opposite end by the quasiperiodic defect-state pump; two nominally identical samples are fabricated and measured under identical conditions.

Experiment 5

Test robustness of the minimal-control quasiperiodic Fibonacci-type end-to-end pump against controlled structural defects in the S-bend implementation. Use N = 11 femtosecond-laser-written glass waveguide arrays of length L = 40 mm with the same dA = 10 µm and dB = 7 µm Fibonacci-type spacing construction as the defect-free pump, and bend only waveguides 2 and 10 using the same two-circular-arc S-bend trajectory with Δx = 3 µm and R ≈ 133 m to interpolate between the two end-localized phason configurations. Introduce local bond defects by replacing selected nearest-neighbor spacings in the otherwise Fibonacci-type array with dD = 8 µm. Measure four defect classes separately: one dA bond replaced by dD; two dA bonds replaced by dD; one dB bond replaced by dD; and two spatially separated dB bonds replaced by dD. For each defect class, inject 780 nm light into the leftmost waveguide i = 1 at z = 0 using a 10× NA = 0.25 objective and image the full 11-site output facet at z = L onto a CCD camera with a matched collection objective. The spatial scope of each measurement is the entire waveguide array, and two nominally identical samples per defect configuration are independently fabricated and measured. The measurement is configured to determine whether the end-to-end transfer mechanism based on a quasiperiodic localized pumping state tolerates specified local changes in inter-waveguide spacing.

Λ-enhanced gray-molasses loading and EIT cooling of neutral atoms in nanophotonic traps

2605.13387
Prediction Quality0.8798
Numeric rel. CRPS0.3748
Coverage @1σ0.5456
Coverage @2σ0.6917
Bool Brier0.0100
Categorical Brier0.0668
Bool/Categorical Accuracy1.0000
Formula Accuracyn/a

Experiment 1

Central concept: test whether Λ-enhanced gray molasses (ΛGM), which combines velocity-selective coherent population trapping with blue-detuned polarization-gradient cooling in a Λ system, can increase loading of an unusually shallow nanophotonic cesium-atom trap compared with conventional red-detuned polarization-gradient cooling (RPGC). The apparatus uses laser-cooled Cs atoms near the evanescent field of a tapered optical nanofiber with nominal waist radius 225 nm. The two-color nanofiber trap uses a fiber-guided red-detuned standing wave at wavelength 935 nm launched from both fiber ends with 55 µW total setting per paper convention, and a fiber-guided blue-detuned running wave at wavelength 685 nm with 7.75 mW; the calculated trap minimum is about 270 nm from the nanofiber surface and the calculated trap depth is 24 µK. A 3D MOT prepares the cloud for 1.5 s using a D2 cooler red-detuned by 2 natural linewidths ΓD2 from the Cs F=4 to F′=5 transition and a D2 repumper resonant with F=3 to F′=4, with counter-propagating σ+ and σ− beams. During a 19 ms transition phase the magnetic-field gradient is ramped to zero, D2 cooling intensity is reduced, and D2 detuning is increased to 11 ΓD2. The loading sequence then applies RPGC on the D2 line for variable durations up to about 50 ms and then switches to ΛGM on the D1 line for variable durations up to about 200 ms. In ΛGM, two phase-coherent light fields couple the two Cs hyperfine ground states to the 6P1/2, F′=4 state; the D1 cooler is blue-detuned from F=4 to F′=4 by Δ1P=4.5 ΓD1, and the second phase-coherent field is generated with an electro-optic modulator near the Cs ground-state hyperfine splitting with a sideband-to-carrier ratio of 1:4. The two-photon detuning is held at the experimentally selected near-resonant value for the loading-time measurement. The spatial scope is the nanofiber-guided dipole trap along the fiber waist, consisting of two diametral one-dimensional arrays of trapping sites with axial period λred/(2 neff), where neff=1.08. For each loading time, the dependent variable is obtained by weak fiber-guided transmission spectroscopy on the D2 F=4 to F′=5 transition; the probe is scanned for 500 µs at approximately 10^-2 times the saturation power, with transverse probe polarization parallel to the plane containing the trapped atoms, and the optical depth is extracted by fitting the transmission spectrum with a saturated Lorentzian.

Experiment 2

Central concept: characterize the cooling action of the ΛGM stage used to load a nanophotonic Cs trap by measuring the temperature of the surrounding untrapped molasses cloud, because lower temperature is expected to increase capture into the very small trap volumes near the optical nanofiber. A Cs MOT is prepared with the D2 cooler and repumper beams as in the nanofiber experiment, followed by the 19 ms magnetic-field and D2-cooling transition ramp and a 45 ms RPGC stage on the D2 transition. ΛGM is then applied on the D1 transition using counter-propagating σ+ and σ− beams coupling the Cs F=3 and F=4 ground states to 6P1/2, F′=4; the D1 cooler is detuned by Δ1P=4.5 ΓD1 from F=4 to F′=4, the second phase-coherent field is generated by an EOM near the hyperfine splitting, and the two-photon detuning Δ2P is either swept across the near-resonance range shown in the paper or set at zero for the duration-dependent measurement. The temperature measurement applies to the untrapped free-space Cs atom cloud in the vicinity of the nanofiber rather than to atoms already bound in the evanescent-field trap. The cloud temperature is measured by time-of-flight imaging after releasing the atoms, with ΛGM durations including a short fixed-duration detuning scan and a duration scan extending to the long-cooling condition used to characterize the final ΛGM temperature.

Experiment 3

Central concept: determine the two-photon detuning condition under which ΛGM most effectively loads atoms into the nanofiber trap, testing the role of the velocity-selective dark state in the small-volume nanophotonic capture process. The same Cs nanofiber trap is used: a 225 nm nominal-radius nanofiber waist, 935 nm red-detuned standing-wave trap field at 55 µW launched from both directions, 685 nm blue-detuned running-wave trap field at 7.75 mW, calculated trap depth 24 µK, and trap sites at two diametral arrays near the nanofiber surface. The atoms are first prepared in the MOT and RPGC sequence, then ΛGM is applied on the D1 line with Δ1P=4.5 ΓD1 and a phase-coherent second field generated by an EOM with sideband-to-carrier ratio 1:4. The independent variable is the ΛGM two-photon detuning Δ2P, scanned over the approximately ±100 kHz range around the hyperfine two-photon resonance. For each detuning, the final nanofiber-trapped optical depth is obtained by fiber-guided weak-probe transmission spectroscopy on the D2 F=4 to F′=5 transition and normalized to the maximum optical depth within the scan; the detuning dependence is fit with a Gaussian to extract the peak position. The measurement samples the same guided-mode interaction region of the nanofiber trap and is sensitive to residual light shifts and magnetic fields through shifts of the optimum dark-state condition.

Experiment 4

Central concept: independently quantify the number of atoms coupled to the nanofiber and the single-atom coupling probability after ΛGM-assisted loading, establishing whether the enhanced loading produces a large guided-mode optical depth in a shallow nanophotonic trap. The sample is a Cs ensemble loaded into the two-color nanofiber trap at the waist of a 225 nm nominal-radius tapered optical fiber, using the MOT, RPGC, and ΛGM sequence described for the loading experiment. The optical depth is measured by scanning a weak nanofiber-guided probe across the Cs D2 F=4 to F′=5 transition at approximately 10^-2 Psat for a 500 µs scan; the probe polarization is transverse and parallel to the plane containing the trapped atoms, and the transmission spectrum is fit with a saturated Lorentzian. The atom number is measured in the same class of loaded ensembles by a depumping method: a weak 1.3 pW nanofiber-guided probe resonant with the Cs D2 F=4 to F′=4 transition, with transverse polarization orthogonal to the plane containing the trapped atoms, is sent as a boxcar-shaped pulse through the fiber; the time-dependent transmission is fit to the optical-pumping model of Ref. [49], equivalently checked by counting missing photons while accounting for branching ratios to the two hyperfine ground states. The guided-mode emission probability is then derived as β=OD/(4N), where OD is the resonant optical depth of the trapped ensemble and N is the atom number. The spatial scope is the nanofiber-waist trap region addressed by the guided probe, integrating over the ensemble along the fiber rather than imaging individual sites.

Experiment 5

Central concept: determine whether ΛGM-assisted loading drives the nanofiber trap into the collisional blockade filling regime, where at most one Cs atom occupies each trap site and the expected site-occupation ceiling is set by light-assisted two-body loss. The nanofiber trap uses the same 935 nm red-detuned standing-wave and 685 nm blue-detuned running-wave fields, forming two diametral one-dimensional arrays of sites above and below the nanofiber. The site spacing used to convert line density to filling factor is λred/(2 neff)=935 nm/(2×1.08), and both diametral arrays are included in the filling calculation. To prepare a high-atom-number ensemble for imaging, the MOT is loaded for 1.2 s, followed by 60 ms of RPGC and 190 ms of ΛGM loading. The trapped ensemble is illuminated through the nanofiber using the D2 EIT-cooling light fields detuned by about 7.3 ΓD2, and fluorescence is imaged perpendicular to the guided-light propagation direction onto an Andor iXon Ultra EMCCD. The image in the analysis is accumulated by repeating the imaging sequence 3000 times with 23 ms integration/probing time per shot. The spatial analysis covers the fiber-waist region visible in the EMCCD image, including the approximately millimeter-scale central portion away from the up-taper where the evanescent trapping intensity decreases; the local line density from fluorescence is combined with an independent total atom-number measurement to infer the local filling factor along the nanofiber.

Experiment 6

Central concept: test whether applying the same free-space ΛGM light fields after loading can act as EIT-assisted cooling for atoms already bound in the shallow nanofiber trap, thereby extending trap storage without relying on reloading from the surrounding cloud. The Cs ensemble is first loaded into the 24 µK two-color nanofiber trap using the MOT, RPGC, and ΛGM loading sequence. After loading, all molasses beams are turned off and the optical depth is measured as a function of hold time in the trap to obtain the passive loss curve. For the active-cooling condition, all molasses beams remain off during an initial waiting time long enough for untrapped atoms in the surrounding cloud to fall below the nanofiber, then the ΛGM beams are turned on again after specified delays such as 45 ms or about 175–195 ms. The ΛGM fields use the D1 Λ system coupling the Cs hyperfine ground states to 6P1/2, F′=4 with the D1 cooler detuned by Δ1P=4.5 ΓD1 and the phase-coherent second field set near the two-photon resonance. The measurement probes only atoms remaining in the nanofiber-guided trap after the waiting interval and tracks the ensemble-integrated guided-mode optical depth by weak D2 F=4 to F′=5 transmission spectroscopy as a function of hold time over hundreds of milliseconds. Exponential fits to OD versus hold time define the passive and active storage time constants.

Experiment 7

Central concept: determine whether free-space ΛGM/EIT-assisted cooling preserves the atom–nanofiber coupling strength during storage by counteracting motional heating in the shallow evanescent-field trap. The same nanofiber-trapped Cs ensemble is prepared using the MOT, RPGC, and ΛGM loading sequence in the two-color trap with 935 nm standing-wave red field and 685 nm running-wave blue field. After loading, the atoms are either held passively with all molasses beams off or held with the D1 ΛGM fields reapplied after the waiting interval used to avoid loading from the falling untrapped cloud. At selected hold times normalized by the passive storage time τp, two independent guided-probe measurements are performed: resonant D2 F=4 to F′=5 transmission spectroscopy gives OD, and D2 F=4 to F′=4 depumping transmission gives atom number N. The coupling strength is calculated as β=OD/(4N), where β is the probability for an excited Cs atom to emit into the guided nanofiber mode. The measurement integrates over the full trapped ensemble addressed by the guided mode and compares the time dependence of β for passive and actively cooled storage conditions.

Experiment 8

Central concept: demonstrate an all-guided-light implementation of EIT cooling in a nanophotonic trap, exploiting the radially decaying nanofiber evanescent field to address radial motional sidebands with co-propagating guided fields rather than external paraxial beams. Cs atoms are first loaded into the same shallow two-color nanofiber trap. After loading, a waiting interval is inserted so that untrapped atoms fall away from the nanofiber, and then two additional phase-coherent nanofiber-guided fields co-propagate through the fiber to establish a Λ system on the Cs D2 line via F′=4. The strong coupling field is blue-detuned from the F=4 to F′=4 transition by Δ1P,EIT,C=2.5 ΓD2 for the storage-time measurement, with guided power in the few-hundred-picowatt range; sidebands are generated by a fiber-based phase modulator with carrier-to-sideband ratio 13:1. The transverse polarization of the EIT-cooling fields is aligned orthogonal to the plane containing the trapped atoms. Passive and active storage curves are measured by recording the nanofiber-guided optical depth versus hold time after loading, with the active guided-EIT condition switched on after a 41 ms delay. The exponential decay of OD over the measured hold interval defines the passive and guided-EIT-cooled storage times for the same nanofiber trap geometry.

Experiment 9

Central concept: identify the cooling resonance and motional frequency associated with the all-guided EIT-cooling mechanism in the nanofiber trap. The loaded Cs atoms are held in the same 24 µK two-color nanofiber-guided trap, and the EIT-cooling fields are two phase-coherent co-propagating D2 guided fields coupling the Cs hyperfine ground states through F′=4. For the two-photon detuning scan, the optical depth is measured after a fixed 20 ms guided-EIT cooling interval while the two-photon detuning Δ2P,EIT is scanned across the resonance; Δ2P,EIT is defined as zero when probe and coupling fields couple the two hyperfine ground states without changing motional quantum number. Negative Δ2P,EIT addresses sideband cooling and positive detuning addresses sideband heating. For the coupling-strength scan, the one-photon detuning Δ1P,EIT,C of the strong coupling field is set to values in the plotted MHz-scale range, the coupling Rabi frequency Ω1P,EIT,C is varied by changing the guided coupling-field power, and the optical depth after EIT cooling is used to locate the maximum. The EIT cooling condition used for comparison is (1/2)[sqrt(Δ1P,EIT,C^2+Ω1P,EIT,C^2)-|Δ1P,EIT,C|]=ν, where Δ1P,EIT,C is the one-photon detuning of the guided coupling field, Ω1P,EIT,C is its Rabi frequency, and ν is the relevant trap oscillation frequency inferred from fitting the optimum Rabi-frequency positions. The measurement samples the nanofiber-trapped ensemble addressed by the guided evanescent fields and is sensitive to radial, azimuthal, and axial motional contributions within the one-dimensional EIT-cooling model.

Experiment 10

Central concept: compare ΛGM and RPGC loading dynamics at the single-site rate-equation level to determine whether the enhanced ΛGM loading is limited by collisional blockade in the nanofiber trap. The nanofiber trap is the same two-color evanescent-field trap with two diametral arrays of sites and an estimated atom-cloud length along the nanofiber of 1.5 mm for counting available trapping sites. The number of available sites is determined using the axial period λred/(2 neff) with λred=935 nm and neff=1.08 and by including the two arrays. The atom number N(t) used for loading-rate extraction is inferred from the measured optical-depth loading curves via N=OD/(4β), using β=1% for this rate estimate. The one-atom loading rate R for each cooling method is obtained from the initial slope dN/dt at the onset of the RPGC or ΛGM loading stage divided by the number of available traps. The one-atom loss rate γ is obtained as γ=1/τ, where τ is the storage time of trapped atoms while applying the respective cooling method. For comparison with collisional blockade, the trap volume V is calculated from the full widths at half maximum of the trap potential in cylindrical coordinates, dr=270 nm, dθ=0.87 rad, dz=150 nm, and the relation V=dr dz dθ r ln[1/(1−η)] sqrt[η/(1−η)], where η=kBT/|U0|, T is the atom temperature, U0 is the trap depth, kB is Boltzmann’s constant, and r is the trap radius from the fiber axis; the Cs two-body loss coefficient κ=10×10^-10 cm^3 s^-1 is used to compute κ/(4V). The regime criterion is γ/2<R<κ/(4V) for collisional-blockade-limited loading.

Vectorial field reconstruction without detecting the field

Experiment 1

Multi-frame phase-shifting digital holography characterization of the undetected vector beam using the same induced-coherence interferometer. The central mechanism is that a 1550 nm idler field from Q1 receives a spatially varying polarization transformation from an m = 2 vortex wave plate/q-plate but remains undetected; after idler overlap at Q2, which-source indistinguishability converts the local idler polarization projection into 810 nm signal interference visibility. The optical hardware consists of two identical 1 mm × 1 mm × 2 mm PPLN nonlinear crystals pumped by a continuous-wave 532 nm single-mode laser, type-0 non-degenerate SPDC at 810 nm signal and 1550 nm idler, 75 mm focal-length relay lenses fP1, fP2, fI1, fI2, fS1, fS2, a 150 mm Fourier lens fk before the sCMOS camera, and 810 ± 10 nm filters before detection. HWP3 is set to 0° to measure the horizontal idler projection α(r) and to 45° to measure the vertical idler projection β(r). For each projection, a piezoelectric actuator with nanometre-scale positioning applies four relative interferometric phase steps θ′ = 0, π/2, π, and 3π/2; one camera interferogram is acquired per phase step, so eight images are used for the two-projection Jones reconstruction. For each camera pixel corresponding to a signal transverse momentum qS and correlated idler component qI, the local interference amplitude is evaluated as A_qI = 2*sqrt([I_qS(3π/2)-I_qS(π/2)]^2 + [I_qS(0)-I_qS(π)]^2)/(I_qS(0)+I_qS(π/2)+I_qS(π)+I_qS(3π/2)), where I_qS(θ′) is the recorded signal intensity at phase step θ′. With HWP3 at 0°, A_qI is interpreted as α_qI; with HWP3 at 45°, A_qI is interpreted as β_qI. The relative phase is evaluated pixelwise as φ_qI = arctan((I_qS(3π/2)-I_qS(π/2))/(I_qS(0)-I_qS(π))). The spatial scope is the camera-resolved far-field signal image covering approximately 0–1000 pixels in x and 0–1000 pixels in y, with the circular beam area used for the reconstructed amplitude and phase maps; the measurement compares both polarization projections over the same transverse field of view.

Experiment 2

Single-shot off-axis holography characterization and quantitative comparison with phase-shifting holography for the same induced-coherence vectorial reconstruction concept. A continuous-wave 532 nm pump drives two identical 1 mm × 1 mm × 2 mm PPLN SPDC sources Q1 and Q2 producing 810 nm signal and 1550 nm idler photons. The Q1 idler is structured at the Fourier plane of a 75 mm/75 mm 4f relay by an m = 2 vortex wave plate/q-plate and then projected by HWP3 at 0° or 45° before overlap with the Q2 idler mode; the idler is not directly detected. The Q1 and Q2 signal fields are recombined at a beam splitter, spectrally filtered by two 810 ± 10 nm filters, Fourier-transformed by fk = 150 mm, and recorded on an sCMOS camera at one image per second. For off-axis holography, no piezo phase stepping is used; instead, a small angular offset between the interfering 810 nm signal fields produces spatial carrier fringes. One interferogram is recorded for each polarization projection, giving two images total for the two-projection vector-field reconstruction. The recorded intensity has the off-axis holographic form I(r)=|EO(r)+ER(r)|^2, where EO(r) and ER(r) are the object and reference signal fields. The Fourier transform is filtered to isolate one displaced first-order term, that term is shifted to the origin, and an inverse Fourier transform gives a complex field whose modulus gives the local projection amplitude and whose argument gives the phase. The spatial scope is the camera-resolved far-field signal image covering approximately 0–1500 pixels in x and 0–1400 pixels in y, evaluated over the reconstructed circular beam region and compared with the phase-shifting reconstruction over its corresponding region of interest. The quantitative comparison evaluates amplitude reconstruction quality, background level inside the region of interest, signal-to-noise ratio, and phase-map degradation area; the paper does not provide more detailed mathematical definitions for these comparison metrics.

Ghost State of Light

Experiment 1

Central concept tested: a single-mode laser-driven optical microcavity with a delayed thermo-optical nonlinearity can steer the intracavity-field relaxation path into the phase-space bottleneck left by a nearby saddle-node bifurcation, producing a long-lived non-stationary optical state that appears as a plateau in transmitted intensity before the unique steady state is reached. The apparatus is a plano-concave Fabry-Pérot microcavity filled with olive oil held by capillary forces. The cavity mirrors are distributed Bragg reflectors with peak reflectance 99.9% at 530 nm; each DBR consists of 11 alternating SiO2 and Ta2O5 layers. One mirror is planar and the other has a laser-written concave feature of 7.5 µm diameter and 180 µm radius of curvature. The mirror separation is fixed at approximately 10 µm using screw-tuned sandwiched mounts separated by stiff rubber. A tunable continuous-wave laser in the 530–540 nm range drives the cavity through a 20x microscope objective, and transmitted light is collected through a second 20x microscope objective and detected with an avalanche photodetector. A mechanical chopper rotating at 20 Hz implements a step in incident laser power from zero to 550 µW. Two laser-cavity detunings are compared on the same single-mode cavity geometry: one chosen so that, after the power step, the driven cavity is close to the saddle-node bifurcation detuning Δb, and one chosen farther from the same bifurcation. The measured observable is the transmitted optical intensity versus time after the power step, over the single spatial optical mode defined by the concave microcavity; no lateral scan is performed. The intended measurement is to determine whether the relaxation trajectory contains a non-stationary intensity plateau and to quantify its duration relative to the step time, with the detuning condition serving as the independent control variable.

Experiment 2

Central concept tested: the thermo-optical memory time of the olive-oil-filled microcavity is the delayed nonlinear-response timescale that can redirect relaxation toward the saddle-node bottleneck. The same plano-concave Fabry-Pérot microcavity is used: DBR mirrors with 99.9% peak reflectance at 530 nm, 11 alternating SiO2/Ta2O5 layers per DBR, a planar mirror facing a concave laser-written mirror feature of 7.5 µm diameter and 180 µm radius of curvature, and an inter-mirror spacing of approximately 10 µm. Olive oil is held between the mirrors by capillary forces. A tunable continuous-wave laser in the 530–540 nm range is coupled into and out of the cavity using 20x microscope objectives, and transmitted intensity is recorded with an avalanche photodetector. A mechanical chopper at 20 Hz supplies a step-like optical excitation. The measurement targets the short-time overshoot in the transmitted-intensity relaxation trajectory produced by the delayed thermo-optical response. The oil thermal relaxation time τ is obtained from the full width at half maximum of that overshoot peak. The measurement is local to the single transverse cavity mode selected by the 7.5 µm concave feature; no spatial scan or multi-position averaging is specified.

Experiment 3

Central concept tested: in the presence of experimental fluctuations, the lifetime of the non-stationary ghost-state plateau in the memory-nonlinear optical cavity should fluctuate from shot to shot and can show scaling associated with diffusion through a nearly flat phase-space bottleneck near a saddle-node bifurcation. The apparatus is the same olive-oil-filled single-mode plano-concave Fabry-Pérot microcavity, using DBR mirrors with 99.9% peak reflectance at 530 nm, 11 alternating SiO2/Ta2O5 layers per DBR, a laser-written concave feature of 7.5 µm diameter and 180 µm radius of curvature, and approximately 10 µm mirror spacing. The cavity is driven by a tunable 530–540 nm continuous-wave laser, coupled and collected with 20x microscope objectives, and measured in transmission with an avalanche photodetector. A 20 Hz mechanical chopper repeatedly applies a power step from zero to the operating laser power, with the laser-cavity detuning set close to the saddle-node bifurcation condition after the step. The data set consists of 1000 independent experimental time traces from the same single-mode cavity geometry. For each trace, the ghost lifetime is defined as the duration of the post-step transmitted-intensity plateau before relaxation proceeds toward the final steady state. A probability density function of the measured lifetimes, expressed in milliseconds, is constructed and fitted over the range where an algebraic dependence is assessed; the expected comparison form is PDF ∝ t^p, where t is the ghost lifetime and p is the fitted exponent.
